is inverse to the composite of the representations
(\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi)
as maps
D(G)\to\mathrm{End}(V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi})
. It is already surjective as it is a bimodule map and
\Phi(\sum_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}\sum_{c,i}c\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{i}\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{c}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{i})=\sum_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}=1\in D(G)
. Therefore it suffices to check that applying the representation (
13
) undoes
\Phi
. Focussing on the block
\mathrm{End}(V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi})
and acting with its image on
c^{\prime}\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{i^{\prime}}\in V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle\Phi(c\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{i}\mathop{{\otimes}}

\displaystyle\delta_{d}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{j}){\triangleright}(c^{\prime}\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{i^{\prime}})={\dim V_{\pi}\over|C_{G}|}\sum_{n\in C_{G}}\pi(n^{-1})_{ji}(\delta_{c}\mathop{{\otimes}}q_{c}nq_{d}^{-1}){\triangleright}(c^{\prime}\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{i^{\prime}})

\displaystyle={\dim V_{\pi}\over|C_{G}|}\sum_{n\in C_{G}}\pi(n^{-1})_{ji}\delta_{c,q_{c}nq_{d}^{-1}c^{\prime}q_{d}n^{-1}q_{c}^{-1}}c\mathop{{\otimes}}\pi^{\prime}(q^{-1}_{c}q_{c}nq_{d}^{-1}q_{c^{\prime}})_{j^{\prime}i^{\prime}}e_{j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime}}\delta_{d,c^{\prime}}c\mathop{{\otimes}}{\dim V_{\pi}\over|C_{G}|}\sum_{n\in C_{G}}\pi(n^{-1})_{ji}\pi^{\prime}(n)_{j^{\prime}i^{\prime}}e_{j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime}}\delta_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}\delta_{d,c^{\prime}}\delta_{j,i^{\prime}}c\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{i}

as required. Here,
c=q_{c}nq_{d}^{-1}c^{\prime}q_{d}n^{-1}q_{c}^{-1}
iff
n^{-1}r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}=q_{d}^{-1}c^{\prime}q_{d}n^{-1}
which is iff
q_{d}r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}q_{d}^{-1}=c^{\prime}
which is iff
c=c^{\prime}
. This is zero unless
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}=\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime}
also. We then used the full orthogonality (
11
) for the group
C_{G}
.

By general arguments as in the group case, it follows that
P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
acts as the identity on
V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
and
V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}^{*}
(and zero on other components). One can also check this explicitly, for example,

\displaystyle P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}{\triangleright}(c\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{i})

\displaystyle={\dim V_{\pi}\over|C_{G}|}\sum_{d\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}\sum_{n\in C_{G}}\Tr_{\pi}(n^{-1})\delta_{d,q_{d}nq_{d}^{-1}cq_{d}n^{-1}q_{d}^{-1}}d\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{j}\pi(\zeta_{c}(q_{d}nq_{d}^{-1})_{ji}

\displaystyle={\dim V_{\pi}\over|C_{G}|}\sum_{n\in C_{G}}\Tr_{\pi}(n^{-1})c\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{j}\pi(n)_{ji}=c\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{i}

since
d=q_{d}nq_{d}^{-1}cq_{d}n^{-1}q_{d}^{-1}
iff
c=q_{d}nq^{-1}_{d}dq_{d}n^{-1}q_{d}^{-1}=q_{d}nr_{c}n^{-1}q_{d}^{-1}=q_{d}r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}q_{d}^{-1}=d
. We used the strong orthogonality relations. Likewise for
f^{v}{\triangleleft}P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}=f^{v}
. ∎

We see that, while
\Phi
clearly sends the identity element or ‘maximally entangled state’ of
V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}\mathop{{\otimes}}V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}^{*}
to
P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
, it also implies a basis of all of
D(G)
broken down into irreps
(\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi)
and elements
\Phi(c\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{i}\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{d}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{j})
for each block. We will need this result for the discussion of ribbon teleportation.

Figure 1
:
Example of a ribbon operator for a ribbon
\xi
starting at
s_{0}=(v_{0},p_{0})
to
s_{1}=(v_{1},p_{1})
.

3
D(G)
ribbon operators

To discuss the physics further, one needs the notion of a ribbon operator. By definition, a ribbon
\xi
is a strip of face width that connects two sites
s_{0}=(v_{0},p_{0})
to
s_{1}=(v_{1},p_{1})

by a sequence of sites (shown dashed) as for example in Figure
1
. We call a ribbon
closed
if its endpoints are at the same site, and
open
if the endpoints are at disjoint sites with no intersection.
Note that there exist ribbons which are neither open nor closed, which end at the same vertex but with different faces, say, but we are not concerned with this case. In Figure
1
we also show an associated ribbon operator
F^{h,g}_{\xi}

acting on the spaces associated to the participating arrows and trivially elsewhere. The ribbon has an edge along which we transport
h
from the initial vertex by conjugation along the path, at
each vertex of which we apply the conjugated
h
in the manner of a vertex operation but only to the cross arrow that comes anticlockwise from the dashed site marker. It follows that if we concatenate ribbon
\xi^{\prime}
following on from ribbon
\xi
then we have the first of

F_{\xi^{\prime}\circ\xi}^{h,g}=\sum_{f\in G}F_{\xi^{\prime}}^{f^{-1}hf,f^{-1}g}\circ F_{\xi}^{h,f};\quad F^{h,g}_{\xi}\circ F^{h^{\prime},g^{\prime}}_{\xi}=\delta_{g,g^{\prime}}F_{\xi}^{hh^{\prime},g},

(15)

where we see the coproduct
\Delta\delta_{g}
of
\mathbb{C}(G)
. The latter implies the adjointness

(F_{\xi}^{h,g})^{\dagger}=F_{\xi}^{h^{-1},g}

(16)

with respect to the inner product of
\mathcal{H}
.

Example 2.6
.

Let the state on the l.h.s. of Figure
1
be
|\psi\rangle
, and take the inner product with another state:

h^{\prime 1}
h^{\prime 2}
h^{\prime 3}
h^{\prime 4}
g^{\prime 1}
g^{\prime 2}
g^{\prime 3}
g^{\prime 4}
|\psi^{\prime}\rangle
:=

\displaystyle\langle\psi^{\prime}|(F_{\xi}^{h,g}|\psi\rangle)

\displaystyle=\delta_{g}(h^{1}h^{2}(h^{3})^{-1}h^{4})\delta_{h^{\prime 1}}(h^{1})\delta_{h^{\prime 2}}(h^{2})\delta_{h^{\prime 3}}(h^{3})\delta_{h^{\prime 4}}(h^{4})\delta_{g^{\prime 1}}(g^{1}h^{-1})

\displaystyle\delta_{g^{\prime 2}}(g^{2}(h^{1})^{-1}h^{-1}h^{1})\delta_{g^{\prime 3}}(g^{3}h^{3}(h^{2})^{-1}(h^{1})^{-1}h^{-1}h^{1}h^{2}(h^{3})^{-1})

\displaystyle\delta_{g^{\prime 4}}(g^{4}h^{3}(h^{2})^{-1}(h^{1})^{-1}h^{-1}h^{1}h^{2}(h^{3})^{-1})

\displaystyle=\delta_{g}(h^{\prime 1}h^{\prime 2}(h^{\prime 3})^{-1}h^{\prime 4})\delta_{h^{\prime 1}}(h^{1})\delta_{h^{\prime 2}}(h^{2})\delta_{h^{\prime 3}}(h^{3})\delta_{h^{\prime 4}}(h^{4})\delta_{g^{1}}(g^{\prime 1}h)

\displaystyle\delta_{g^{2}}(g^{\prime 2}(h^{1})^{-1}hh^{1})\delta_{g^{3}}(g^{\prime 3}h^{3}(h^{2})^{-1}(h^{1})^{-1}hh^{1}h^{2}(h^{3})^{-1})

\displaystyle\delta_{g^{4}}(g^{\prime 4}h^{3}(h^{2})^{-1}(h^{1})^{-1}hh^{1}h^{2}(h^{3})^{-1})

\displaystyle=(\langle\psi^{\prime}|F_{\xi}^{h^{-1},g})|\psi\rangle

and by (
15
),
(F^{h,g}_{\xi})^{\dagger}F^{h,g}_{\xi}=F^{h,g}_{\xi}(F^{h,g}_{\xi})^{\dagger}=F^{e,g}_{\xi}
.

Ribbon operators of the form
F^{e,g}_{\xi}
produce only a scalar
\delta_{g}(\cdots)
when applied to a lattice state. It is easy to see that

[F^{e,g}_{\xi},F^{e,g^{\prime}}_{\xi^{\prime}}]=0

(17)

for all
g,g^{\prime}\in G
and ribbons
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
.

Another important property of ribbon operators is that closed, contractible ribbons admit a trivial action of the corresponding ribbon operator on a vacuum state.

Example 2.7
.

An example of a closed ribbon operator
F_{\zeta}^{h,g}
from site
(v,p)
going anticlockwise back to itself is shown in Figure
2
. We compare this with the following sequence of operations (i)
\delta_{g}{\triangleright}
at site
(v,p_{0})
, (ii)
h{\triangleright}
at
v
, (iii)
(h^{1})^{-1}hh^{1}{\triangleright}
at
v_{1}
(iv)
(h^{2})^{-1}(h^{1})^{-1}hh^{1}h^{2}{\triangleright}
at
v_{2}
, and (v)
h^{3}(h^{2})^{-1}(h^{1})^{-1}hh^{1}h^{2}(h^{3})^{-1}{\triangleright}
at
v_{3}
. The final results differ only on the initial arrows
(h^{4},g^{8})
where the ribbon sends these to
(h^{4},g^{8}h^{-1}hg^{-1}h^{-1}g)
(given the
\delta_{g}
) while the sequence by contrast sends these to
(hg^{-1}h^{-1}gh^{4},g^{8}h^{-1})
. Thus, the two act the same as long as the state they act on forces
\delta_{g,e}
. This is true for a vacuum state where
\delta_{g}{\triangleright}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\delta_{g}\Lambda^{*}{\triangleright}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\delta_{g,e}\Lambda^{*}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\delta_{g,e}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
and where
F^{h,g}_{\zeta}
can be viewed as starting with
\delta_{g}{\triangleright}
, as does our sequence. We have
h{\triangleright}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=h\Lambda{\triangleright}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\Lambda{\triangleright}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
similarly, hence the action of the sequence (i)-(v) on the vacuum is
\delta_{g,e}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
. We conclude that
F^{h,g}_{\zeta}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\delta_{g,e}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
.

Figure 2
:
(a) Example of a circular ribbon starting at
(v,p)
and going anticlockwise, and (b) proof that this acts trivially on a vacuum state

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\xi
be a ribbon between sites
s_{0}=(v_{0},p_{0})
and
s_{1}=(v_{1},p_{1})
. Then

[F_{\xi}^{h,g},f{\triangleright}_{v}]=0,\quad[F_{\xi}^{h,g},\delta_{e}{\triangleright}_{p}]=0,

for all
v\notin\{v_{0},v_{1}\}
and
p\notin\{p_{0},p_{1}\}
.

f{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}\circ F_{\xi}^{h,g}=F_{\xi}^{fhf^{-1},fg}\circ f{\triangleright}_{s_{0}},\quad\delta_{f}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}\circ F_{\xi}^{h,g}=F_{\xi}^{h,g}\circ\delta_{h^{-1}f}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}},

f{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}\circ F_{\xi}^{h,g}=F_{\xi}^{h,gf^{-1}}\circ f{\triangleright}_{s_{1}},\quad\delta_{f}{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}\circ F_{\xi}^{h,g}=F_{\xi}^{h,g}\circ\delta_{fg^{-1}hg}{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}

for all ribbons where
s_{0},s_{1}
are disjoint, i.e. when
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
share neither vertices or faces.

Proof.

We refer to the example in Figure
1
to be concrete, but the arguments are general. (1) Commutation of the ribbon with
f{\triangleright}
at sites across from the main path is automatic because the ribbon acts on the states on the cross arrows (
g^{1},\dots,g^{4}
in the example) like a vertex operator on the main path, which has an opposite relative orientation to a vertex at the other end of the relevant cross arrow. Hence the two actions are from opposite sides and commute.
f{\triangleright}
between
h^{1},h^{2}
changes these to
h^{1}f^{-1},fh^{2}
in the illustration which does not change the product when it comes to parts of a subsequent ribbon operator at later vertices. It also changes
g^{2}
to
g^{2}f^{-1}
. When we then apply the ribbon operator this changes to
g^{2}f^{-1}(h^{1}f^{-1})^{-1}h^{-1}h^{1}f^{-1}=g^{2}(h^{1})^{-1}h^{-1}h^{1}f^{-1}
which is what we get if we apply the ribbon first and then
f{\triangleright}
at this vertex. The same cancellation applies at other vertices on the main path other than the endpoints.

(2) The action of
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}
at a face depends on the cyclic order determined by the vertex part of the site; commutation only holds in general if we chose this correctly or if we restrict to
\delta_{e}
as stated (this disagrees with
[
BSW11
]
). For faces on the other side of the main path
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}
has the form of
\delta_{f}(...h_{i}...)
where the
...
are states on arrows unaffected by the ribbon. The ribbon op does not change
h_{i}
so commutes with
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}
. The other relevant faces are those in the body of the ribbon itself and we look at all three in detail. (i) The face bounded by
g^{1},h^{1},g^{2}
and an unkown
x
has
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}=\delta_{f}(g^{1}h^{1}(g^{2})^{-1}x^{-1})
in some cyclic order. But if we apply the ribbon first then
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}=\delta_{f}(g^{1}h^{-1}h^{1}(g^{2}(h^{1})^{-1}h^{-1}h^{1})^{-1}x^{-1})
in the same cyclic order, which we see is the same unless we started at
g^{2}
. (ii) The face bounded by
g^{2},h^{2},h^{3},g^{3}
has
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}=\delta_{f}(g^{2}h^{2}(h^{3})^{-1}(g^{3})^{-1})
in some cyclic order. But if we apply the ribbon first then
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}=\delta_{f}(g^{2}(h^{1})^{-1}h^{-1}h^{1}h^{2}h^{3}(g^{3}h^{3}(h^{2})^{-1}(h^{1})^{-1}h^{-1}h^{1}h^{2}(h^{3})^{-1})^{-1})
in the same order which again cancels (but only for the order shown). (iii) The face bounded by
g^{3},g^{4}
and unknowns
x,y
say has

\delta_{f}{\triangleright}=\delta_{f}(g^{3}(g^{4})^{-1}x^{-1}y)
, say, in some cyclic order. If we apply the ribbon first then
g^{3}
is replaced by
g^{3}w
for a certain expression
w
but so is
g^{4}
, so
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}
is the same as long as we do not start at
g^{4}
.

(3) We have four remaining cases and again we refer to Figure
1
to be concrete. (i)
f{\triangleright}
at vertex
v_{0}
sends
(h^{1},g^{1})
to
(fh^{1},g^{1}f^{-1})
(the other two arrows are also changed but this commutes with the ribbon operation). Applying
F^{fhf^{-1},fg}_{\xi}
changes this to
(fh^{1},g^{1}f^{-1}(fhf^{-1})^{-1})
with a factor
\delta_{fg}(fh^{1}\cdots)
. If we apply
F^{h,g}_{\xi}
first then we have
(h^{1},g^{1}h^{-1})
and a factor
\delta_{g}(h^{1}\cdots)
and applying
f{\triangleright}
turns the former to
(fh^{1},g^{1}h^{-1}f^{-1})
, which is the same. (ii) Similarly,
f{\triangleright}
at vertex
v_{1}
sends
h^{4}
to
h^{4}f^{-1}
(the action on other, unmarked, arrows commutes with the ribbon operator). The ribbon operator
F^{h,gf^{-1}}_{\xi}
then gives a factor
\delta_{gf^{-1}}(\cdots h^{4}f^{-1})
. If we apply the ribbon first, we have
\delta_{g}(\cdots h^{4})
and then
f{\triangleright}
gives the same as before. (iii)
\delta_{h^{-1}f}{\triangleright}
at
v_{0}
gives factor
\delta_{h^{-1}f}((g^{1})^{-1}...)
(for three unmarked arrows around the rest of the face) and the ribbon then gives a factor sends gives a factor
\delta_{g}(h^{1}\cdots)
. It also acts on
g^{1}
. If we apply the ribbon first, then this gives
\delta_{g}(h^{1}\cdots)
and changes
g^{1}
to
g^{1}h^{-1}
. Then applying
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}
gives a factor
\delta_{f}((g^{1}h^{-1})^{-1}\cdots)
, which is the same. (iv)
\delta_{fg^{-1}hg}{\triangleright}
at
v_{1}
gives a factor
\delta_{fg^{-1}hg}(\cdots g^{4}h^{4})
for two unmarked arrows at the start of the face). The ribbon then imposes
\delta_{g}(zh^{4})
where
z
is the product along the ribbon main path up to
h^{4}
(in our case,
h^{1}h^{2}(h^{3})^{-1}
). If we apply the ribbon first then
g^{4}
gets changed to
g^{4}z^{-1}h^{-1}z
and then
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}
gives
\delta_{f}(\cdots g^{4}z^{-1}h^{-1}zh^{4})
, which is the same, given the
\delta_{g}(zh^{4})
factor. ∎

This means that
F_{\xi}^{h,g}
commutes with all terms of the Hamiltonian except those at
s_{0},s_{1}
, where the nontrivial commutation relations will be used to create a quasiparticle at
s_{0}
and its antiparticle at
s_{1}
.
In this sense, a ribbon operator is a generalisation of the creation operators discussed in Section
1
. We briefly consider so-called triangle operators, as they will be useful in future proofs.

Definition 2.9
.

The direct-triangle and dual-triangle operators
T^{g}_{\tau}
and
L^{h}_{\tau^{*}}
respectively are defined by

We also show how a ribbon can be built as a sequence of triangle operations.

Here the dual lattice inherits an orientation by anticlockwise rotation of the unique arrow that crosses a dual arrow. If the flow around either triangle is clockwise then
h
or
g
enters as shown, otherwise their opposite version much as before.
For the dual triangle, this is the same as the arrow pointing inwards towards the vertex. Triangle operations can be viewed as atomic instances of ribbon operators, where
the start and end are adjacent sites, namely the associated ribbons are
F_{\tau}^{h,g}=T^{g}_{\tau}
and
F_{\tau^{*}}^{h,g}=\delta_{g,e}L_{\tau^{*}}^{h}
respectively and convolving these via (
15
) gives the composite
F_{\xi}^{h,g}
. However, triangle operators are not open ribbons due to
s_{0},s_{1}
not being disjoint, so they have different commutation relations from those in Lemma
2.8
, which we study in full later. It is clear that we have have algebras
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}:=\mathrm{span}\{T^{g}_{\tau}\ |\ g\in G\}{\cong}\mathbb{C}(G)
and
\mathcal{A}_{\tau^{*}}:=\mathrm{span}\{L^{h}_{\tau^{*}}\ |\ h\in G\}{\cong}\mathbb{C}G
in view of the composition rules

T^{g}_{\tau}\circ T^{g^{\prime}}_{\tau}=\delta_{g,g^{\prime}}T^{g}_{\tau},\quad L^{h}_{\tau^{*}}\circ L^{h^{\prime}}_{\tau^{*}}=L^{hh^{\prime}}_{\tau^{*}}.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
be a vacuum vector on a
plane
\Sigma
. Let
\xi
be a ribbon between fixed sites
s_{0}:=(v_{0},p_{0}),s_{1}:=(v_{1},p_{1})
and

|\psi^{h,g}\rangle:=F_{\xi}^{h,g}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle.

(1)
|\psi^{h,g}\rangle
is independent of the choice of ribbon
between fixed sites
s_{0},s_{1}
.

(2) The space

\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1}):=\{|\psi\rangle\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}\ |\ A(v)|\psi\rangle=B(p)|\psi\rangle=|\psi\rangle,\quad\forall v\notin\{v_{0},v_{1}\},p\notin\{p_{0},p_{1}\}\}

is spanned by
\{|\psi^{h,g}\rangle\ |\ h,g\in G\}
.

(3) When sites
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
are disjoint,
\{|\psi^{h,g}\rangle\ |\ h,g\in G\}
is an
orthogonal basis of
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
. We call this the ‘group basis’ of
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
.

(4)
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})\subset\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}
inherits actions at disjoint sites
s_{0},s_{1}
,

f{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\psi^{h,g}\rangle=|\psi^{fhf^{-1},fg}\rangle,\quad\delta_{f}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\psi^{h,g}\rangle=\delta_{f,h}|\psi^{h,g}\rangle

f{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}|\psi^{h,g}\rangle=|\psi^{h,gf^{-1}}\rangle,\quad\delta_{f}{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}|\psi^{h,g}\rangle=\delta_{f,g^{-1}h^{-1}g}|\psi^{h,g}\rangle

isomorphic to the left and right regular representation of
D(G)
by
|\psi^{h,g}\rangle\mapsto\delta_{h}g
.

Proof.

(1) Acting on the vacuum, a contractible, closed ribbon acts
trivially as we have illustrated. Then if
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
are two ribbons
between the same sites, we regard the composite of the reverse of

\xi^{\prime}
with
\xi
as a contractible, closed ribbon, as
\Sigma
is a
plane. We then use equation (
15
).

(2) We leave this proof to Appendix
8.M
, as it is lengthy and similar in some respects to
[
BM-D08
]
.

(3) This proof can be found in
[
BSW11
]
, but we include it to clarify that it applies only when
s_{0},s_{1}
are disjoint. Thus,

\langle\psi^{h,g}|\psi^{h^{\prime},g^{\prime}}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|(F^{h,g}_{\xi})^{\dagger}F^{h^{\prime},g^{\prime}}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{h^{-1},g}_{\xi}F^{h^{\prime},g^{\prime}}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\delta_{g,g^{\prime}}\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{h^{-1}h^{\prime},g}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

and, if
s_{0},s_{1}
are disjoint,

\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{h,g}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|(\delta_{e}{\triangleright}_{p_{1}})^{\dagger}F^{h,g}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|\delta_{e}{\triangleright}_{p_{1}}F^{h,g}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{h,g}_{\xi}\delta_{g^{-1}hg}{\triangleright}_{p_{1}}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

so that
\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{h,g}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=0
if
h\neq e
. When
h=e
,

\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{e,g}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|(k{\triangleright}_{v_{1}})^{\dagger}F^{e,g}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{e,gk}_{\xi}k^{-1}{\triangleright}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{e,gk}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

for every
k
, from which we deduce that
\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{e,g}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
is independent of

g
. Since
\sum_{g\in G}F^{e,g}_{\xi}=\text{id}
, it follows that
\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{h,g}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\frac{\delta_{h,e}}{|G|}

and hence that

\langle\psi^{h,g}|\psi^{h^{\prime},g^{\prime}}\rangle=\delta_{g,g^{\prime}}\langle\mathrm{vac}|F^{h^{-1}h^{\prime},g}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle\ =\frac{1}{|G|}\delta_{h,h^{\prime}}\delta_{g,g^{\prime}}.

Combined with (2),

\{|\psi^{h,g}\rangle\ |\ h,g\in G\}
is then an orthogonal basis of
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
.

If
s_{0}
,
s_{1}
are not disjoint then Lemma
2.8
no longer applies, and the commutation relations are different. For example, if
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
are joined by a direct triangle
\tau
then
F^{h,g}_{\tau}=T^{g}_{\tau}
so
\{|\psi^{h,g}\rangle\ |\ h,g\in G\}
are no longer orthogonal.

(4) This follows from the commutation relations in Lemma
2.8
at
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
using
f{\triangleright}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
and
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\delta_{f,e}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
replacing
f
as modified by the commutation relations. Making the identification with
D(G)
we compare the
s_{0}
action with the left regular representation
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}(\delta_{h}g)=\delta_{f}\delta_{h}g=\delta_{f,h}\delta_{h}g
and
f{\triangleright}(\delta_{h}g)=f\delta_{h}g=\delta_{fhf^{-1}}fg
using the
D(G)
commutation relations. The right regular representation is made into a left action via the antipode, so
\delta_{f}{\triangleright}(\delta_{h}g)=\delta_{h}g\delta_{f^{-1}}=\delta_{h}\delta_{gf^{-1}g^{-1}}g=\delta_{f,g^{-1}h^{-1}g}g
and
f{\triangleright}(\delta_{h}g)=\delta_{h}gf^{-1}
. These match the stated
D(G)
actions at the end sites. ∎

Remark 2.11
.

The above Proposition
2.10
is known in the literature, albeit in different forms, see
[
BSW11
]
, and is included to be precise in our set up. It assumes that we begin with a vacuum state
|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
on
\Sigma
. It is immediate, however, that the same arguments apply for a state
|\vartheta\rangle
which is merely
locally
vacuum – that is,
B(p)|\vartheta\rangle=A(v)|\vartheta\rangle=|\vartheta\rangle
for
v,p
at sites along the ribbon path and in the region between if we change the ribbon path. Thus, (1) now becomes more precisely that
|\vartheta^{h,g}\rangle:=F^{h,g}_{\xi}|\vartheta\rangle
is invariant under choice of ribbons
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
between fixed sites
s_{0}
,
s_{1}
iff the composite of
\xi
with reversed
\xi^{\prime}
forms a closed, contractible ribbon
\xi^{\prime\prime}
, and where
A(v)|\vartheta\rangle=B(p)|\vartheta\rangle=|\vartheta\rangle
for all
p
and
v
adjacent to
\xi^{\prime\prime}
and in the region enclosed by
\xi^{\prime\prime}
. The intuition is that the ribbons may be smoothly deformed into one another, and thus leave the state invariant by previous arguments. The subspace
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}^{\prime}(s_{0},s_{1})
is then defined in the natural way, ignoring excitations outwith the local neighbourhood of consideration, and actions are inherited on the sites
s_{0}
,
s_{1}
in the identical manner to
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
.
This locality of the Hamiltonian
H
allows us to create quasiparticles at distance without being concerned about the compounding effects: they may be considered entirely separately. While we don’t refer to it explicitly, this remark applies to the corollaries and applications throughout the Chapter in this context.

The last part of Proposition
2.10
implies a new basis of
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
in terms of the quasiparticle content at the two ends.

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
\xi
be an open ribbon from
s_{0}
to
s_{1}
. Then
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
has an alternative ‘quasiparticle basis’ consisting for each irrep
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi
of
D(G)
of the elements

|u,v;\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi\rangle={\dim V_{\pi}\over|C_{G}|}F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi;u,v}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle;\quad F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi;u,v}:=\sum_{n\in C_{G}}\pi(n^{-1})_{ji}F_{\xi}^{c,q_{c}nq_{d}^{-1}}

where
u=(c,i)
and
v=(d,j)
with
c,d\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}
and
i,j=1,\cdots\dim V_{\pi}
.

Proof.

Here
|u,v;\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi\rangle=\tilde{\Phi}(e_{u}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{v})
by which we mean
\Phi(e_{u}\mathop{{\otimes}}e^{v})
in Theorem
2.5
, where
e_{u}=c\mathop{{\otimes}}e^{i}
and
f^{v}=\delta_{d}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{j}
are basis elements of
V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
and
V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}^{*}
respectively, then identified with an element of
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
by the inverse of the last part of Proposition
2.10
. ∎

These states behave for the left site action
{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}
on
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
according to a quasiparticle state labelled by basis element
e_{u}
and for the right action at
s_{1}
according to an anti-quasiparticle state labelled by the dual basis element
f^{v}
. Recall that we view the left site action
{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}
as a right one via the antipode
S
of
D(G)
. The ribbon operators
F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi;u,v}
that create these states from the vacuum are also of interest in their own right and it is claimed in
[
BM-D08
]
that they form a basis of the space of operators that commute with almost all
A(v)
and
B(p)
in the same way that
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
is defined.

Corollary 2.13
.

If
|\psi\rangle\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
and we detect in it a quasiparticle of type
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi
at
s_{0}
by nonzero projection
P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\psi\rangle
then

P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\psi\rangle=|\psi\rangle{\triangleleft}_{s_{1}}P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi},

hence we also automatically detect it at
s_{1}
, and vice-versa. In particular, the state

|\mathrm{Bell},\xi\rangle=\sum_{h\in G}F_{\xi}^{h,e}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

has a nonzero projection
P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\mathrm{Bell},\xi\rangle=|\mathrm{Bell},\xi\rangle{\triangleleft}_{s_{1}}P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}\neq 0
for all
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi
.

Proof.

This is essentially a block version of teleportation. A general state is highly entangled in a superposition between the different particle types,

|\psi\rangle=\sum_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}\sum_{u,v}\phi(\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi,u,v)\tilde{\Phi}(e_{u}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{v})

where, as above,
\tilde{\Phi}:\mathrm{End}(V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi})\to\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
denotes
\Phi
combined with the inverse of the identification in Proposition
2.10
. Here
\{e_{u}\}
are a basis of
V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
and
\{f^{u}\}
a dual basis. Applying
P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}
and
{\triangleleft}_{s_{1}}P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
becomes via the bimodule properties
respectively
P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}{\triangleright}e_{u}
and
f^{v}{\triangleleft}P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
. But these projections are zero unless
(\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})=(\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi)
, in which case they act as the identity, as in the proof of Theorem
2.5
. Hence

P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\psi\rangle=\sum_{u,v}\phi(\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi,u,v)\tilde{\Phi}(e_{u}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{v})=|\psi\rangle{\triangleleft}_{s_{1}}P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}.

In particular,

P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|u,v;\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi,\xi\rangle=|u,v;\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi,\xi\rangle{\triangleleft}_{s_{1}}P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}=\delta_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{\prime},\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}\delta_{\pi^{\prime},\pi}|u,v;\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi,\xi\rangle.

(18)

For the ‘block Bell state’, we consider
1_{D(G)}=\sum_{h\in G}\delta_{h}\mathop{{\otimes}}e
which map to
\sum_{h}|\psi^{h,e}\rangle
according to the last part of Proposition
2.10
. On the other hand, this is
\sum_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
by Lemma
2.4
and hence each term is the image under
\Phi
of
\sum e_{u}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{u}
in Theorem
2.5
. Thus,

|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle=\sum_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}\sum_{u}\tilde{\Phi}(e_{u}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{u})=\sum_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}\sum_{u}|u,u;\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi\rangle

and from (
18
) we have

P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\mathrm{Bell},\xi\rangle=|\mathrm{Bell};\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi,\xi\rangle=|\mathrm{Bell},\xi\rangle{\triangleleft}_{s_{1}}P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}\neq 0

where

|\mathrm{Bell};\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi,\xi\rangle=\sum_{u}\tilde{\Phi}(e_{u}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{u})=\sum_{u}|u,u;\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi\rangle

is the claimed nonzero state projected out from
|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle
.
∎

We recall that in teleportation one has an entangled ‘Bell state’,
\sum_{i}|v_{i}\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}\langle v_{i}|
for a basis and dual basis of a Hilbert space, and if we apply from the left a projection
|v_{1}\rangle\langle v_{1}|
say then the state collapses to
|v_{1}\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}\langle v_{1}|
so that the right factor is an eigenstate if we apply
|v_{1}\rangle\langle v_{1}|
from the right. Equivalently, if we evaluate against any
\langle\psi|
on the left then the result is
\langle\psi|
in the right factor. We see a similar phenomenon with the block
V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}\mathop{{\otimes}}V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}^{*}
in place of
|v_{i}\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}\langle v_{i}|
. In the
D(\mathbb{Z}_{n})
case discussed below, each block will be 1-dimensional so that we are then a bit closer to the standard case.

We can also potentially look inside each block, i.e. for each fixed
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi
, regard
|\mathrm{Bell};\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi,\xi\rangle\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
as a ‘mini Bell state’ that can similarly transport a single particle state across the ribbon. We saw in the proof above that this is
\sum_{u}\Phi(e_{u}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{u})=P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
mapped over to this space by the inverse of the identification in the last part of Proposition
2.10
. We can also write

|\mathrm{Bell};\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi,\xi\rangle={\dim V_{\pi}\over|C_{G}|}W_{\xi}^{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle;\quad W_{\xi}^{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}:=\sum_{u}F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi;u,u}.

(19)

so that the ‘ribbon trace operator’
W_{\xi}^{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
has the physical interpretation of creating a maximally entangled quasiparticle/anti-quasiparticle pair (the mini Bell state) of only the specified type
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi
. The issue for teleportation of a single quasiparticle state vector using a such mini Bell state would be how, in a quantum computer, to create a single particle state or its dual and evaluate it against
e_{u}
at
s_{0}
or against
f^{u}
at
s_{1}
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\xi:s_{0}\to s_{1}
and
\xi^{\prime}:s_{1}\to s_{2}
be open ribbons. Then

F_{\xi^{\prime}\circ\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi;u,v}=\sum_{w}F_{\xi^{\prime}}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi;w,v}\circ F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi;u,w}

Proof.

We have using (
15
),

\displaystyle F_{\xi^{\prime}\circ\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi;(c,i),(d,j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in C_{G}}\pi(n^{-1})_{ji}F_{\xi^{\prime}\circ\xi}^{c,q_{c}nq_{d}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f\in G}\sum_{n\in C_{G}}\pi(n^{-1})_{ji}F_{\xi^{\prime}}^{f^{-1}cf,f^{-1}q_{c}nq_{d}^{-1}}\circ F_{\xi}^{c,f}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}\sum_{k}\sum_{m,n\in C_{G}}\pi((m^{-1}n)^{-1})_{jk}\pi(m^{-1})_{ki}F_{\xi^{\prime}}^{b,q_{b}m^{-1}nq_{d}^{-1}}F_{\xi}^{c,q_{c}mq_{d}^{-1}}

where we uniquely factorised
f^{-1}q_{c}=q_{b}m^{-1}
in terms of some
b\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}
and
m\in C_{G}
. We then change variables to
n^{\prime}=m^{-1}n
and recognise the answer with
w=(b,k)
.
∎

This reflects that
F_{\xi}^{\prime}
are a kind of (nonAbelian) Fourier transform of the original
F_{\xi}
with convolution as in (
15
) becoming multiplication. Invertibility of Fourier transform implies that the space spanned by such operators is the same as the space spanned by the original
F_{\xi}
, now organised according to the quasiparticle type.
In addition, we have:

Lemma 2.15
.

W^{e,\pi}_{\xi}\circ W^{e,\pi^{\prime}}_{\xi}=W^{e,\pi\otimes\pi^{\prime}}_{\xi}

Proof.

Using (
19
), Corollary
2.12
and (
15
) in that order,

\displaystyle W^{e,\pi}_{\xi}\circ W^{e,\pi^{\prime}}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,n^{\prime}\in G}\mathrm{Tr}_{\pi}(n^{-1})F^{e,n}_{\xi}\mathrm{Tr}_{\pi^{\prime}}(n^{\prime-1})F^{e,n^{\prime}}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,n^{\prime}\in G}\mathrm{Tr}_{\pi}(n^{-1})\mathrm{Tr}_{\pi^{\prime}}(n^{\prime-1})\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}F^{e,n}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\mathrm{Tr}_{\pi\otimes\pi^{\prime}}(n^{-1})F^{e,n}_{\xi}

which we recognise as stated.
∎

We will also need the following.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
\xi:s_{0}\to s_{1}
be an open ribbon. Then
W_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}{}^{\dagger}=W_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{*},\pi^{*}}
where
\pi^{*}
is the conjugate unitary representation of
C_{G}
and
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{*}=\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{-1}
equipped with
r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{*}}=r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}^{-1}
and
q:\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{-1}\to G
given by
q_{c^{-1}}=q_{c}
for all
c\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}
.

Proof.

Here

F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi;(c,i),(d,j)}{}^{\dagger}=\sum_{n\in C_{G}}\overline{\pi(n^{-1})_{ji}}F_{\xi}^{c^{-1},q_{c}nq_{d}^{-1}}=\sum_{n\in C_{G}}\pi^{*}(n^{-1})_{ij}F_{\xi}^{c^{-1},q_{c}nq_{d}^{-1}}=F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{-1},\pi^{*};(c^{-1},j),(d^{-1},i)}

where
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{*}
is the
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{-1}
with the basepoint and
q
function data as stated and the same
C_{G}
. We now take the trace by summing over
c=d
and
i=j
. ∎

Note that it could be that
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}=\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{-1}
as a set but is not
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{*}
due to a different base point (this happens for the order two conjugacy class of
S_{3}
).

The last ingredient we need for applications is a generalisation of the space
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
. If
s_{0},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}
are
n+1
sites, define the subspace

\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}):=\{|\psi\rangle\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}\ |\ A(v)|\psi\rangle=B(p)|\psi\rangle=|\psi\rangle,\forall v\notin\{v_{0},v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\},p\notin\{p_{0},p_{1},\cdots,p_{n}\}\}.

Lemma 2.17
.

Given a
D(G)
model on a borderless planar lattice
\Sigma
, let
s_{0},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}
be
n+1
disjoint sites. Then

\dim(\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}))=|G|^{2n}

with an orthogonal basis

\{|\psi^{\{h^{1},h^{2},\cdots,h^{n}\},\{g^{1},g^{2},\cdots,g^{n}\}}\rangle\ |\ h^{1},h^{2},\cdots,h^{n},g^{1},g^{2},\cdots,g^{n}\in G\}

generalising the group basis of
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
. There is another orthogonal basis that is the equivalent generalisation of the quasiparticle basis.

Proof.

As we saw in the proof of Proposition
2.10
, the only operations which take the vacuum to states with excitations only at any two sites, say
s_{0},s_{1}
, are ribbon operators along a ribbon
\xi:s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1}
. Now, let
A
be a complete graph, with sites
s_{0},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}
as vertices. We then have a contribution to
\dim(\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}))
of
|G|^{2}=\dim(\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1}))
from an edge in A between
s_{0},s_{1}
, corresponding to some ribbon
\xi:s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1}
; we can give this the group basis with labels
\{h^{1},g^{1}\ |\ h^{1},g^{1}\in G\}

or the equivalent quasiparticle basis. Edges in
A
between other vertices/sites contribute similarly, so for example there are another
|G|^{2}
orthogonal ribbon operators along the ribbon
\xi^{\prime}:s_{1}\rightarrow s_{2}
, which multiplies with the initial
|G|^{2}
from
\xi
. However, by Lemma
2.8
, if we have already counted the operators along ribbons
\xi:s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1}
and
\xi^{\prime}:s_{1}\rightarrow s_{2}
then any ribbon operator
F^{h,g}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}
for
\xi^{\prime\prime}:s_{0}\rightarrow s_{2}
has a decomposition into ribbons along
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
iff
\xi^{\prime\prime}
is
isotopic to
\xi^{\prime}\circ\xi
. Therefore the only edges which contribute are between vertices which have no alternative path along previously visited edges.
In particular, we define
T
as any maximally spanning tree on
A
. Then
\dim(\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}))
receives contributions from exactly the
n
edges in
T
, and we may for example give the group basis with labels
\{h^{i},g^{i}\ |\ h^{i},g^{i}\in G\}
from each edge.
∎

We note that while the dimensions multiply, it is not true that
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})
can be presented as
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{n-1},s_{1})
where the tensor product is along each edge in
T
. This is because, for example, ribbon operators
F^{h,g}_{\xi}
and
F^{h^{\prime},g^{\prime}}_{\xi^{\prime}}
meet at the endpoint
s_{1}
and need not commute. On the other hand, if we have some disjoint subsets of
\{s_{0},\cdots,s_{n-1}\}
then the tensor product of the logical spaces associated to each subset form a subspace. For example

\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})\mathop{{\otimes}}\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{2},s_{3})\subset\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

by sending
F_{\xi}^{h,g}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}F_{\xi^{\prime}}^{h^{\prime},g^{\prime}}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle\mapsto F_{\xi}^{h,g}\circ F_{\xi^{\prime}}^{h^{\prime},g^{\prime}}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
.

4
Reduction to Abelian model for
G=\mathbb{Z}_{n}

In this section, we verify that everything above reduces correctly to the Abelian case already covered in Section
1
via the Fourier correspondence (
1
). Here
D(\mathbb{Z}_{n})=\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{Z}_{n})\mathop{{\otimes}}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{Z}_{n}{\cong}\mathbb{C}.\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n}=\mathbb{C}[g,h]/\langle g^{n}-1,h^{n}-1\rangle
and we recall that we set
q=e^{2\pi\imath\over n}
. Clearly, at a face

g{\triangleright}=\sum_{m}q^{m}\delta_{m}{\triangleright}=\sum_{m}q^{m}\delta_{m,i+j-k-l}=q^{i+j-k-l}

if the state around the face is
|i\rangle,|j\rangle,|k\rangle,|l\rangle
with orientations as displayed before. This no longer depends on the starting point. Moreover
h{\triangleright}
around a vertex is the action of
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
so acts as before. This clearly gives gives
A(v)
as before and
B(p)=\delta_{0}{\triangleright}={1\over n}\sum_{k}g^{k}{\triangleright}
as before.

The vacuum degeneracy of the Abelian model is straightforward to calculate.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed, orientable surface, and let
G=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. Then

\dim(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}_{vac})=n^{2k},

where
k
is the genus of
\Sigma
.

Proof.

The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2k}
.
\mathbb{Z}^{2k}
is a

2k
-biproduct of
\mathbb{Z}
in the category of groups, so

\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}^{2k},\mathbb{Z}_{n})\cong\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}_{n})^{2k}
. Now,

|\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}_{n})|=n
, so
|\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}^{2k},\mathbb{Z}_{n})|=n^{2k}
. The
G
-action is trivial, so we are done.
∎

For representations, the conjugacy classes are singletons
\{i\}
, say, with isotropy group all of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, with irrep
\pi_{j}
say. The carrier space is 1-dimensional and the irrep is

g{\triangleright}\{i\}=\sum_{m}q^{m}\delta_{m,i}\{i\}=q^{i}\{i\},\quad h{\triangleright}\{i\}=q^{j}\{i\}

as employed before. Projectors simplify to those from Section
1
. Thus,

\displaystyle P_{j}

\displaystyle={\dim V_{\pi}\over|G|}\sum_{g}(\Tr_{j}g^{-1})g=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k}q^{-jk}g^{k}

\displaystyle P_{\{i\},j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{i}\mathop{{\otimes}}P_{j}=\delta_{i}\mathop{{\otimes}}{1\over n}\sum_{l\in G}q^{-jl}g^{l}\cong P_{i}\mathop{{\otimes}}P_{j}={1\over n^{2}}\sum_{k,l}q^{-(ik+jl)}h^{k}g^{l}=P_{ij}

by Fourier isomorphism between
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{Z}_{n}
and
\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{Z}_{n})
.

The ribbon operators are now labelled as
F_{\xi}^{a,b}
say, where
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
and have a simpler form. For example,

for the ribbon in Figure
1
. Concatenation of ribbon operators simplifies to:

F_{\xi^{\prime}\circ\xi}^{a,b}=\sum_{f\in G}F_{\xi^{\prime}}^{a,b-f}\circ F_{\xi}^{a,f}.

(20)

The commutation relations in Lemma
2.8
simplify to

h{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}\circ F^{a,b}_{\xi}=F_{\xi}^{a,b+1}\circ h{\triangleright}_{s_{0}},\quad g{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}F^{a,b}_{\xi}=q^{a}F^{a,b}_{\xi}g{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}

h{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}\circ F^{a,b}_{\xi}=F_{\xi}^{a,b-1}\circ h{\triangleright}_{s_{0}},\quad g{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}F^{a,b}_{\xi}=q^{-a}F^{a,b}_{\xi}g{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}

i.e. these ‘q-commute’. For example,

g{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}\circ F^{a,b}_{\xi}=\sum_{m}q^{m}\delta_{m}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}\circ F^{a,b}_{\xi}=\sum_{m}q^{m}F^{a,b}\delta_{m-a}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}={1\over n}\sum_{m,k}q^{m}F^{a,b}_{\xi}q^{-(m-q)k}g^{k}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}.

The sum over
m
forces
k=1
which then gives the answer stated. Consequently, on states
|\psi^{a,b}\rangle=F^{a,b}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
we just have that

h{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\psi^{a,b}\rangle=|\psi^{a,b+1}\rangle,\quad g{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\psi^{a,b}\rangle=q^{a}|\psi^{a,b}\rangle

which commute, and similarly at
s_{1}
.

For the quasiparticle basis, the relevant ribbon operator and its adjoint are

F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}i,j}:=\sum_{k}q^{-jk}F_{\xi}^{i,k},\quad F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}i,j}{}^{\dagger}=F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}-i,-j}

where we omit
u,v
as these are trivial and the
i,j
play the role of
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi
respectively in the construction of
P_{ij}
above. We see that
F^{\prime}_{\xi}
is just a Fourier transform in the second argument, which takes convolution to multiplication so that (
20
) becomes

F^{{}^{\prime}i,j}_{\xi^{\prime}\circ\xi}=\sum_{k}q^{-j(k-l)-jl}\sum_{l}F^{i,k-l}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ F^{i,l}_{\xi}=F^{\prime i,j}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ F^{\prime i,j}_{\xi}.

(21)

Also, since there are no
u,v
indices,
W_{\xi}^{i,j}=F^{{}^{\prime}i,j}_{\xi}
. The following three subsections show how the above might be used in practice to perform operations relevant to quantum computation.

Abelian Bell state and teleportation

According to our general theory and our calculations above, the state which is maximally entangled between the particle types is

|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle=\sum_{i,j}{1\over n}F_{\xi}^{{}^{\prime}i,j}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j,k}q^{-jk}|\psi^{i,k}\rangle=\sum_{i}F^{i,0}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=\sum_{i}|\psi^{i,0}\rangle,

where the second-to-last step is the Fourier transform. Here
\langle\mathrm{Bell};\xi|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle=n
. For a concrete example, consider

s_{0}
s_{1}
|f\rangle
|k\rangle
|l\rangle
|p\rangle
|m\rangle
=
|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle=\sum_{i}F_{\xi}^{i,0}
\delta_{0}(k+l+m)\sum_{i}
s_{0}
s_{1}
|f+i\rangle
|k\rangle
|p-i\rangle
|l\rangle
|m\rangle
|a\rangle
|b\rangle
|c\rangle
|a\rangle
|b\rangle
|c\rangle
|d\rangle
|d\rangle

for a ribbon
\xi:s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1}
, where
s_{0}=(v_{0},p_{0}),s_{1}=(v_{1},p_{1})
and a generic term in
|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
with relevant arrow values
|a\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}\cdots\mathop{{\otimes}}|p\rangle
as shown. We also know that

|i,j\rangle:=|\mathrm{Bell};i,j,\xi\rangle=P_{ij}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle=|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle{\triangleleft}_{s_{1}}P_{ij}={1\over n}W_{\xi}^{i,j}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

are the ‘mini-Bell’ states associated to each
i,j
. In our case (as there are no
u,v
indices) these have no internal substructure as an entangled sum of internal states and we just regard them as a basis of
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
as we vary
i,j
. To illustrate how this goes explicitly, consider
P_{ij}={1\over n^{2}}\sum_{x,y}q^{-ix-jy}g^{x}h^{y}
as above, acting at
s_{0}
, say. Then, renaming the dummy index
i
in
|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle
as
z
,

\displaystyle P_{ij}

\displaystyle{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle={1\over n^{2}}\sum_{x,y,z}q^{-ix-jy}\delta_{0,k+l+m}q^{x(f+z+y-c-b+a-y)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad|f+z+y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|k+y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|d-y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|a-y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|p-z\rangle

\displaystyle={1\over n}\sum_{y}q^{-jy}\delta_{0,k+l+m}|i+c+b-a+y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|k+y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|d-y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|a-y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|p-i+f-c-b+a\rangle

\displaystyle={1\over n}\sum_{y}q^{-jy}\delta_{0,k+l+m}|i+f+y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|k+y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|d-y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|a-y\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|p-i\rangle

\displaystyle={1\over n}\sum_{y}q^{-jy}\delta_{y,k+l+m}|f+i\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|k\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|d\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|a\rangle\mathop{{\otimes}}|p-i\rangle

\displaystyle={1\over n}\sum_{y}q^{-jy}F_{\xi}^{i,y}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle={1\over n}W_{\xi}^{i,j}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

for the affected arrows located in line with the previous diagram. The sum over
x
forced the value
z=i-f+c+b-a
for the second equality. We then used that
\delta_{0}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}
acts as the identity on the vacuum so that
f+c+b-a=0
around the face
p_{0}
for the third equality. Likewise, we use that
h^{-y}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}
is the identity on the vacuum for the fourth (this shifts the original values
f,k,d,a
around the vertex
v_{0}
by
\mp y
). We then recognise the action of
W_{\xi}^{i,j}
as expected. Similarly for
{\triangleleft}_{s_{1}}P_{ij}
.

We now explain our teleportation point of view in this Abelian case. Here
\{|i,j\rangle\}
are a basis of
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
and we have seen that
P_{ij}(s_{0})
applied to
|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle
collapses the ribbon state to
|i,j\rangle
, and a quasi-particle of type
(i,j)
now occupies
s_{0}
. This is a local operation at
s_{0}
but the resulting state when measured at
P_{-i,-j}(s_{1})={\triangleleft}_{s_{1}}P_{ij}
is also an eigenstate and detects a quasiparticle of type
(-i,-j)
locally at
s_{1}
. Here the right action of
P_{ij}
is the left action of
S(P_{ij})
, where
S
is the antipode of the group algebra of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n}
. Although the details are not the same as usual quantum teleportation, we follow the same principle of using a maximally entangled state to transfer information along the length of an extended object, our case the ribbon. We can use this to transmit any state vector in the vector space spanned by the particle types, i.e. a vector
\vec{\psi}=(\psi_{ij})
. We set up a state
|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle
and apply the operator
\sum_{i,j}\psi_{ij}P_{ij}(s_{0})
to it locally at
s_{0}
. This results in

|\psi\rangle:=\sum_{i,j}\psi_{ij}P_{ij}(s_{0})|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle=\sum_{i,j}\psi_{ij}|i,j\rangle\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})

as a ribbon state that encodes our vector
\vec{\psi}
. We can then read off the latter at the other end by applying the operator
P_{-i,-j}(s_{1})
locally at
s_{1}
, where

\displaystyle P_{-i,-j}(s_{1})|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=|\psi\rangle{\triangleleft}_{s_{1}}P_{ij}=P_{ij}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|\psi\rangle=\sum_{k,l}\psi_{kl}P_{ij}(s_{0})|k,l\rangle=\psi_{ij}P_{i,j}(s_{0})|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle

This is the component of
|\psi\rangle
that contains the
\psi_{ij}
coefficient. It is also equal to
\psi_{ij}P_{-i,-j}(s_{1})|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle
making it clear that we can extract the coefficient by local operations at
s_{1}
.

While such a teleportation scheme is possible when the projectors
P_{ij}
can be applied to the lattice, in reality such projectors can only be applied probabilistically, by performing measurements.
In particular, assuming that application of projectors can be performed deterministically in general has grave complexity-theoretic consequences, such as allowing NP-complete problems to always be solved in polynomial time on a quantum computer
[
Aar05
]
.
This means that, while the above scheme is illustrative of the entanglement between sites
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
, it is unclear how to leverage this property to be computationally useful when the superposition may only be collapsed by a measurement.

Quasiparticle creation and transportation redux

In other words, we have the mini-Bell state
