Quasiparticle braiding

This section gives an
example of braiding on the lattice, and relates it to the braiding of irreducible representations of
D(G)
given at the start of Section
1
. We do not prove explicitly that all such lattice braidings correspond to braids in the representation category,
but the broad arguments are easy to see. Let
\xi:s_{0}\to s_{1}
be the following ribbon acting on a vacuum state
|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
,

|n\rangle
|m\rangle
|k\rangle
s_{0}
s_{1}
|l\rangle
|o\rangle
|p\rangle
|q\rangle

where we have labelled the relevant edges
|k\rangle
to
|q\rangle
as shown.
W^{0,-j}_{\xi}
creates quasiparticles
e_{-j}
,
e_{j}
at
s_{0}
,
s_{1}
,
and takes the vacuum to

|n\rangle
Z^{j}|m\rangle
Z^{j}|k\rangle
Z^{j}|l\rangle
|o\rangle
|p\rangle
|q\rangle

Also consider another ribbon operator
W^{-i,0}_{\xi^{\prime}}
for
\xi^{\prime}:s_{2}\to s_{3}
, creating
m_{-i}
,
m_{i}

quasiparticles at
s_{2},s_{3}
according to

Z^{j}|m\rangle
Z^{j}|k\rangle
Z^{j}|l\rangle
|q\rangle
s_{2}
s_{3}
|n\rangle
|o\rangle
|p\rangle

The combined effect of these is the state
|\psi\rangle:=W^{-i,0}_{\xi^{\prime}}\otimes W^{0,-j}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

Z^{j}|m\rangle
Z^{j}|k\rangle
Z^{j}|l\rangle
X^{i}|q\rangle
|n\rangle
|o\rangle
|p\rangle

Now let
\xi^{\prime\prime}
be a ribbon rotating anti-clockwise from

s_{1}
back to
s_{1}
around the face of
s_{3}
, according to

|n\rangle
Z^{j}|m\rangle
Z^{j}|k\rangle
Z^{j}|l\rangle
|o\rangle
|p\rangle
X^{i}|q\rangle
s_{1}

Acting on
|\psi\rangle
, we move the
e_{j}
quasiparticle at
s_{1}
around the
m_{i}
at
s_{3}
using
W^{0,-j}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}
and resulting in

Z^{j}|m\rangle
Z^{j}|k\rangle
Z^{j}|l\rangle
Z^{j}X^{i}|q\rangle
Z^{-j}|n\rangle
Z^{-j}|o\rangle
Z^{j}|p\rangle

Now use
Z^{j}X^{i}=q^{ij}X^{i}Z^{j}
on
|q\rangle
so that the
Z^{\pm j}
operators that make up

W^{0,-j}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}
act on
|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
. But the latter is a face operator

g^{-j}{\triangleright}
around the face of
s_{3}
and acts trivially on the vacuum. Hence the effect of
W^{0,-j}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}
on
|\psi\rangle
is to send

|\psi\rangle\mapsto q^{ij}|\psi\rangle

as expected for the braiding of
m_{i}
with
e_{j}
. To visualise this braiding, we should think in terms
of worldlines to take account of the temporal aspect: we first create the
quasiparticles, and then transport one around the other. We identify
the map above with

\Psi_{m_{i},e_{j}}\circ\Psi_{e_{j},m_{i}}:e_{j}\otimes m_{i}\rightarrow e_{j}\otimes m_{i}

and have braided the worldline of the

e_{j}
quasiparticle around that of the
m_{i}
quasiparticle and back
again.

While this is only one instance of braiding, any ribbon operator on the plane which forms a closed loop around another occupied site
will admit a similar braiding, as the same argument from above applies but taking a product of vertex and face operators, rather than just
g^{-j}{\triangleright}
in this example. We assert that the state at the occupied site will always admit commutation relations such that
the appropriate phase factor is produced.

5
Details for
D(S_{3})
and applications

S_{3}
is the smallest nonAbelian group. We let
S_{3}
be generated by
u=(12)
,
v=(23)
with relations
u^{2}=v^{2}=e
and
uvu=vuv
(
=w=(13)
). This has three irreducible representations:

1,\quad\sigma=\mathrm{sign},\quad\tau;\quad\sigma\mathop{{\otimes}}\sigma=1,\quad\sigma\mathop{{\otimes}}\tau=\tau\mathop{{\otimes}}\sigma=\tau,\quad\tau\mathop{{\otimes}}\tau=1\oplus\sigma\oplus\tau

where
\tau
is the only 2-dimensional one and
\mathrm{sign}=-1
on
u,v,w
and
+1
otherwise. The irreps of
D(S_{3})
are given by pairs
(\mathcal{C},\pi)
, where
\mathcal{C}

is a conjugacy class in
S_{3}
and
\pi
is an irrep of the centraliser
of a distinguished element
r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}
in
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. an isotropy subgroup, and we also need to fix
q_{c}
for each
c\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}
such that
c=q_{c}r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}q_{c}^{-1}
. We take these as follows:

1.

The trivial conjugacy class
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}=\{e\}
,
r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}=q_{c}=e
and
C_{G}=S_{3}
, giving exactly 3 chargeons
(\{e\},1)
,
(\{e\},\sigma)
and
(\{e\},\tau)
as
D(S_{3})
irreps.

2.

\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}=\{u,v,w\}
,
r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}=u
,
q_{u}=e,q_{v}=w,q_{w}=v
and
C_{G}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{e,u\}
, giving
(\{u,v,w\},1)
and
(\{u,v,w\},-1)
as 2 irreps of
D(S_{3})
, where we indicate the representation
\pi_{-1}(u)=-1
of
C_{G}
.

3.

\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}=\{uv,vu\}
, with
r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}=uv
,
q_{uv}=e
,
q_{vu}=v
and
C_{G}=\mathbb{Z}_{3}=\{e,uv,vu\}
, giving
(\{uv,vu\},1)
,
(\{uv,vu\},\omega)
,
(\{uv,vu\},\omega^{*})
as 3 irreps of
D(S_{3})
, where
\omega=e^{2\pi\imath\over 3}
and we indicate irreps
\pi_{\omega}(uv)=\omega
and
\pi_{\omega^{*}}(uv)=\omega^{-1}
of
C_{G}
.

Thus, there are 8 irreps of
D(S_{3})
. To describe the projectors, we denote the conjugacy class
\mathcal{C}
by its chosen element
r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}
as shorthand, for example
P_{u,\pi}:=P_{\{u,v,w\},\pi}
. The chargeons have projectors

P_{1}=\frac{1}{6}\sum_{g}g,\quad P_{\sigma}=\frac{1}{6}(e-u-v-w+uv+vu),\quad P_{\tau}=\frac{1}{6}(2e-uv-vu)

in
\mathbb{C}S_{3}
with actual
D(S_{3})
projectors
P_{e,1}=\delta_{e}\otimes P_{1},P_{e,\sigma}=\delta_{e}\otimes P_{\sigma},P_{e,\tau}=\delta_{e}\otimes P_{\tau}
. The fluxion projectors are

\displaystyle P_{u,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{c\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}\delta_{c}\mathop{{\otimes}}q_{c}\Lambda_{C_{G}}q_{c}^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{u}\otimes(e+u)+\delta_{v}\otimes(e+v)+\delta_{w}\otimes(e+w))

\displaystyle P_{uv,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(\delta_{uv}+\delta_{vu})(e+uv+vu)

along with
P_{e,1}
from before which can be viewed as either. The remaining projectors after a short computation are

\displaystyle P_{u,-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{u}\otimes(e-u)+\delta_{v}\otimes(e-v)+\delta_{w}\otimes(e-w))

\displaystyle P_{uv,\omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(\delta_{uv}\otimes(e+\omega^{-1}uv+\omega vu)+\delta_{vu}\otimes(e+\omega uv+\omega^{-1}vu))

\displaystyle P_{uv,\omega^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(\delta_{uv}\otimes(e+\omega uv+\omega^{-1}vu)+\delta_{vu}\otimes(e+\omega^{-1}uv+\omega vu))

On a lattice
\Sigma
where each edge has an associated state in
\mathbb{C}S_{3}
,
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
has the quasiparticle basis
|u,v;\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi\rangle
from Corollary
2.12
, where unlike the
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
case

u=(c,i),v=(d,j)
can have different
i,j
as not all irreps are 1-dimensional. To avoid a clash with group elements of
S_{3}
, we will refer to the pairs
(c,i),(d,j)
directly. We again refer to
\mathcal{C}
by its representative. Then in our case, the ribbon operators required to create these bases from vacuum for each chargeon are

\displaystyle F^{{}^{\prime}e,1}_{\xi}=\sum_{n\in S_{3}}F^{e,n}_{\xi}=\text{id},\quad F^{{}^{\prime}e,\sigma}_{\xi}=\sum_{n\in S_{3}}\mathrm{sign}(n)F^{e,n}_{\xi},\quad F^{{}^{\prime}e,\tau;i,j}_{\xi}=\sum_{n\in S_{3}}\tau(n^{-1})_{ji}F^{e,n}_{\xi}

The last of these is the only case with
i,j
indices as the other
\pi
are 1-dimensional. Similarly, for fluxions:

\displaystyle F^{{}^{\prime}u,1;c,d}_{\xi}=F^{c,q_{c}q_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}+F^{c,q_{c}uq_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi},\quad F^{{}^{\prime}uv,1;c,d}_{\xi}=F^{c,q_{c}q_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}+F^{c,q_{c}uvq_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}+F^{c,q_{c}vuq_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}

where in the first case have indices
c,d\in\{u,v,w\}
and in the second case
c,d\in\{uv,vu\}
. The remaining quasiparticle basis operators are

\displaystyle F^{{}^{\prime}u,-1;c,d}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=F^{c,q_{c}q_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}-F^{c,q_{c}uq_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}

\displaystyle F^{{}^{\prime}uv,\omega;c,d}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=F^{c,q_{c}q_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}+\omega^{-1}F^{c,q_{c}uvq_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}+\omega F^{c,q_{c}vuq_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}

\displaystyle F^{{}^{\prime}uv,\omega^{*};c,d}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=F^{c,q_{c}q_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}+\omega F^{c,q_{c}uvq_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}+\omega^{-1}F^{c,q_{c}vuq_{d}^{-1}}_{\xi}

with corresponding indices as before.

We will mainly need the traces
W_{\xi}^{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
of these defined in (
19
). Up to normalisation, these are just the
P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
already computed but converted to ribbon operators according to the last part of Proposition
2.10
. For chargeons these come out as

W^{e,1}_{\xi}=\text{id},\quad W^{e,\sigma}_{\xi}=\sum_{n\in S_{3}}\mathrm{sign}(n)F^{e,n}_{\xi},\quad W^{e,\tau}_{\xi}=\sum_{j}F^{{}^{\prime}e,\tau;j,j}_{\xi}=2F^{e,e}_{\xi}-F^{e,uv}_{\xi}-F^{e,vu}_{\xi}.

For fluxions we have

\displaystyle W^{u,1}_{\xi}=\sum_{c\in\{u,v,w\}}F^{c,e}_{\xi}+F^{c,c}_{\xi},\quad W^{uv,1}_{\xi}=\sum_{c\in\{uv,vu\}}F^{c,e}_{\xi}+F^{c,uv}_{\xi}+F^{c,vu}_{\xi}

and the other ones are

\displaystyle W^{u,-1}_{\xi}=\sum_{c\in\{u,v,w\}}F^{c,e}_{\xi}-F^{c,c}_{\xi}

\displaystyle W^{uv,\omega}_{\xi}=F^{uv,e}_{\xi}+F^{vu,e}_{\xi}+\omega(F^{uv,vu}_{\xi}+F^{vu,uv}_{\xi})+\omega^{-1}(F^{uv,uv}_{\xi}+F^{vu,vu}_{\xi})

\displaystyle W^{uv,\omega^{*}}_{\xi}=F^{uv,e}_{\xi}+F^{vu,e}_{\xi}+\omega(F^{uv,uv}_{\xi}+F^{vu,vu}_{\xi})+\omega^{-1}(F^{uv,vu}_{\xi}+F^{vu,uv}_{\xi})

Note that the
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}=\{uv,vu\}
class is self-inverse but its elements are not self-inverse, so
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{*}
is the same class
\mathcal{C}
but with
r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}^{*}}=vu
and
q^{*}_{uv}=q_{vu}=v,q^{*}_{vu}=q_{uv}=e
. Hence Lemma
2.16
says that

W^{uv,\omega}_{\xi}{}^{\dagger}=W^{vu,\omega^{*}}_{\xi}=\sum_{n}\pi_{\omega^{*}}(n^{-1})(F^{uv,vnv^{-1}}+F^{vu,n})=W^{uv,\omega}_{\xi}

so this works out as self-adjoint (as one can also check directly). Similarly for
W^{uv,\omega^{*}}_{\xi}
, and more obviously for the other cases.

The maximally entangled state is then

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Bell};\xi\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big({1\over 6}(W_{\xi}^{e,1}+W_{\xi}^{e,\sigma}+2W_{\xi}^{e,\tau})+{1\over 2}(W_{\xi}^{u,1}+W_{\xi}^{u,-1})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+{1\over 3}(W_{\xi}^{uv,1}+W_{\xi}^{uv,\omega}+W_{\xi}^{uv,\omega^{*}})\Big)|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in S_{3}}F_{\xi}^{h,e}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

as required by Corollary
2.13
, the first expression being as a sum of 8 mini Bell states.

Protected qubit system using
S_{3}
ribbons

Here, we provide a concrete construction of a protected logical qubit within the

D(S_{3})
Kitaev model, elaborating on ideas in
[
WLP09
]
. Let
\Sigma
be a lattice in the vacuum state.
Let
\xi
be a ribbon between sites
s_{0}:=(v_{0},p_{0})
and
s_{1}:=(v_{1},p_{1})
.

s_{0}
s_{1}

This particular choice of ribbon and sites is just for illustrative purposes; any open ribbon will do. We focus initially on the chargeon sector with
W_{\xi}^{\tau}:=W_{\xi}^{e,\tau}
. If we apply this to the vacuum the lattice is now occupied by quasiparticles
\pi
and
\pi^{*}
at
sites
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
. Next, let
\xi^{\prime}:s_{2}\to s_{3}
be another ribbon and apply the ribbon operator
W^{\tau}_{\xi^{\prime}}

s_{2}
s_{3}

We call this state
|0_{L}\rangle:=W_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\tau}\circ W_{\xi}^{\tau}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
for reasons which will become clear.
|0_{L}\rangle\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})
, and now
\tau
quasiparticles occupy the lattice at sites
s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}
, which is obvious as

P_{\tau}{\triangleright}_{s_{i}}W_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\tau}\circ W_{\xi}^{\tau}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=W_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\tau}\circ W_{\xi}^{\tau}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
for all
i
.

Next, let
\xi^{\prime\prime}:s_{0}\to s_{2}
connect across as

s_{2}
s_{0}

and apply the ribbon operator
W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}
to
|0_{L}\rangle
, defining
|1_{L}\rangle:=W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}|0_{L}\rangle
. We claim that
1_{L}\rangle
still has only
\tau
excitations at
s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}
. We check this by expanding
P_{\tau}
and
W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}
:

\displaystyle P_{\tau}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}(2e{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}-uv{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}-vu{\triangleright}_{s_{0}})(F^{e,e}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,u}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,v}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,w}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}+F^{e,uv}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}+F^{e,vu}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}(2(F^{e,e}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,u}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,v}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,w}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}+F^{e,uv}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}+F^{e,vu}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}})e{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle-(F^{e,vu}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,w}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,u}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,v}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}+F^{e,e}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}+F^{e,vu}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}})uv{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle-(F^{e,uv}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,v}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,w}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,u}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}+F^{e,vu}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}+F^{e,e}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}})vu{\triangleright}_{s_{0}})

\displaystyle=(F^{e,e}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,u}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,v}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}-F^{e,w}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}+F^{e,uv}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}+F^{e,vu}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}})\frac{1}{6}(2e{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}-uv{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}-vu{\triangleright}_{s_{0}})

\displaystyle=W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\circ P_{\tau}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}

by Lemma
2.8
. Therefore

\displaystyle P_{\tau}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|1_{L}\rangle

\displaystyle=P_{\tau}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\circ W^{\tau}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ W^{\tau}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

\displaystyle=W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\circ P_{\tau}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}W^{\tau}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ W^{\tau}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

\displaystyle=W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\circ P_{\tau}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}|0_{L}\rangle=W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}|0_{L}\rangle=|1_{L}\rangle

and an identical calculation applies at
s_{1}
.

The states
|0_{L}\rangle
and
|1_{L}\rangle
are therefore indistinguishable by local projectors, as the orthogonality of projectors shown in Lemma
2.4
means that for all
P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
,
P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}{\triangleright}_{s_{i}}|0_{L}\rangle=P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}{\triangleright}_{s_{i}}|1_{L}\rangle=0
,
\forall s_{i}
iff
\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi\neq e,\tau
, and

P_{e,\tau}{\triangleright}_{s_{i}}|0_{L}\rangle=|0_{L}\rangle
,
P_{e,\tau}{\triangleright}_{s_{i}}|1_{L}\rangle=|1_{L}\rangle
.
A physical explanation is that the
\sigma
quasiparticles generated by
W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}
at sites
s_{0},s_{2}
‘fuse’ with the extant
\tau
quasiparticles, as we have
\sigma\otimes\tau=\tau
.
Now,
|0_{L}\rangle
and
|1_{L}\rangle
are orthogonal since

\displaystyle\langle 0_{L}|1_{L}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|W^{\tau}_{\xi}{}^{\dagger}\circ W^{\tau}_{\xi^{\prime}}{}^{\dagger}\circ W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\circ W^{\tau}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ W^{\tau}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\circ W^{\tau}_{\xi}\circ W^{\tau}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ W^{\tau}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ W^{\tau}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{vac}|W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\circ W^{\tau\otimes\tau}_{\xi^{\prime}}\circ W^{\tau\otimes\tau}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

by (
17
), Lemma
2.15
and Lemma
2.16
. By the arguments of Lemma
2.17
,
W^{\tau\otimes\tau}_{\xi^{\prime}}W^{\tau\otimes\tau}_{\xi}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
has no support in
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{2})
, while
W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
has no support in
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1})
or
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{2},s_{3})
, and so

\langle 0_{L}|1_{L}\rangle=0.

Thus,
\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}_{L}:=\mathrm{span}(\{|0_{L}\rangle,|1_{L}\rangle\})
is a 2-dimensional subspace of
\hbox{{$\mathcal{L}$}}(s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})
that is degenerate under
H
. We call
\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}_{L}
a
logical qubit
on the lattice.
By similar arguments as for the vacuum in Section
1
, any state in
\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}_{L}
is ‘topologically protected’; local errors leave the state unaffected. In this case, the two types of errors which
are undetectable and affect
\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}_{L}
are (a) loops enclosing at least one occupied site and (b) ribbon operators extending between occupied sites. Therefore, quasiparticles should be placed at distant locations
to minimise errors.

We then identify
W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}
with
X_{L}
, the logical
X
gate, which is justified as

W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\circ W^{\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}=W^{\sigma\otimes\sigma}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}=W^{1}_{\xi^{\prime\prime}}=\text{id}

by Lemma
2.15
. Therefore,
X_{L}
is involutive as desired for any implementation of a qubit computation within the model, for example by ZX-calculus based on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as a quasiFrobenius algebra. Clearly, we can obtain any
X_{L}
basis rotation on the logical qubit by exponentiation. In
[
WLP09
]
it is argued that we can in fact acquire universal quantum computation by an implementation of the logical Hadamard, entangling gates and measurements. For completeness, we outline some aspects of these further steps in Appendix
8.N
.

3
Aspects of general
D(H)
models

The Kitaev model is known to generalise with
\mathbb{C}G
replaced by any finite-dimensional Hopf algebra
H
with antipode
S
obeying
S^{2}=\text{id}
(which over
\mathbb{C}
or another field of characteristic zero is equivalent to
H
semisimple or cosemisimple). Although less well studied, that one can obtain topological invariants as a version of the Turaev-Viro invariant was shown in
[
BK12
,
Meu17
]
. That one has an action of the Drinfeld quantum double
D(H)

[
Dri87
]
at each site is more immediate and was first noted in
[
BMCA13
]
. We just replace the group action
g{\triangleright}
by
h{\triangleright}
acting in the tensor product representation with factors in order going around the vertex as in Figure
3
, which now depends on where
p
is located. We likewise replace the action of
\delta_{g}
by
a{\triangleright}
for
a\in H^{*}
and likewise just take the tensor product action around the face in the order depending on where
v
is located. We use the Hopf algebra regular and coregular representations

h{\triangleright}g=hg\quad\mathrm{or}\quad h{\triangleright}g=gSh;\quad a{\triangleright}g=a(g_{1})g_{2}\quad\mathrm{or}\quad a{\triangleright}g=a(Sg_{2})g_{1}

(22)

with the first choice if the arrow is outbound for the vertex /in the same direction as the rotation around the face. Here
\Delta g=g_{1}\mathop{{\otimes}}g_{2}
(sum understood) denotes the coproduct
\Delta:H\to H\mathop{{\otimes}}H
and
a{\triangleright}
is a right action of
H^{*}
viewed as a left action of
H^{*op}
. The antipode
S:H\to H
is characterised by
(Sh_{1})h_{2}=h_{1}Sh_{2}=1{\epsilon}(h)
for all
h\in H
, where
{\epsilon}\in H^{*}
is the counit. We refer to
[
Maj95
]
for more details.

We have also used better conventions for
D(H)
, namely the double cross product construction introduced by the 2nd author in
[
Maj90
]
. Here
D(H){\cong}H^{*op}{\bowtie}H
, where
H
left acts on
H^{*}
and
H^{*}
left acts on
H
by the coadjoint actions

h{\triangleright}a=a_{2}\langle h,(Sa_{1})a_{3})\rangle,\quad h{\triangleleft}a=h_{2}\langle a,(Sh_{1})h_{3}\rangle

with the left action of
H^{*}
viewed as a right action of
H^{*op}
. The numerical suffices denote iterated coproducts (sums understood) and
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
is the duality pairing or evaluation. These then form a matched pair of Hopf algebras
[
Maj90
]
and give the Drinfeld double explicitly as
[
Maj95
, Thm 7.1.1]
,

(a\mathop{{\otimes}}h)(b\mathop{{\otimes}}g)=b_{2}a\mathop{{\otimes}}h_{2}g\langle Sh_{1},b_{1}\rangle\langle h_{3},b_{3}\rangle,\quad\Delta(a\mathop{{\otimes}}h)=a_{1}\mathop{{\otimes}}h_{1}\mathop{{\otimes}}a_{2}\mathop{{\otimes}}h_{2}.

S(a\mathop{{\otimes}}h)=S^{-1}a_{2}\mathop{{\otimes}}Sh_{2}\langle h_{1},a_{1}\rangle\langle Sh_{3},a_{3}\rangle,\quad\hbox{{$\mathcal{R}$}}=\sum_{a}f^{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}1\mathop{{\otimes}}1\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{a},

where we also give the factorisable quasitriangular structure. Here
\{e_{a}\}
is a basis of
H
and
\{f^{a}\}
is a dual basis. We will also sometimes employ a subalgebra notation where
h,a
are viewed in
D(H)
with cross relations
ha=a_{2}h_{2}\langle Sh_{1},a_{1}\rangle\langle h_{3},a_{3}\rangle
and
\hbox{{$\mathcal{R}$}}=\sum_{a}f^{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{a}
.
While this much is clear, explicit properties of ribbon operators have not been much studied as far as we can tell even for
S^{2}=\text{id}
, and we do so here. Moreover, we will explore how much can be done without this semisimplicity assumption.

From Hopf algebra theory, we will particularly need that every finite-dimensional Hopf algebra
H
has, uniquely up to normalisation, a left integral element
\Lambda\in H
such that
h\Lambda={\epsilon}(h)\Lambda
and a right-invariant integral map
\int\in H^{*}
such that
(\int h_{1})h_{2}=1\int h
for all
h
. Ditto with left-right swapped. In the semisimple case in characteristic zero the integrals can be normalised so that
{\epsilon}(\Lambda)=\int 1=1
, are both left and right integrals at the same time, and obey
\int hg=\int gh
and
\Delta\Lambda=\mathrm{flip}\Delta\Lambda
(here
\int=\Tr_{H}/\dim H
is the normalised trace in the left regular representation), see
[
Sch95
]
for an account (the general theory underlying this goes back to the work of Larson and Radford). If we denote irreps of
H
by
(\pi,V_{\pi})
then analogously to the group case, one has a complete orthogonal set of central idempotents
P_{\pi}
given by

P_{\pi}=\dim(V_{\pi})\Lambda_{1}\Tr_{\pi}(S\Lambda_{2})

(23)

whereby
P_{\pi}H=HP_{\pi}{\cong}\mathrm{End}V_{\pi}
. Note that
\sum_{\pi}P_{\pi}=\Lambda_{1}\int S\Lambda_{2}\dim H=1
as part of the Frobenius structure where
\Lambda
is currently normalised so that
\int\Lambda=1/\dim H
compared to usual normalisation in
[
Sch95
,
Maj21
]
. We omit the proof but part of the theory is the orthogonality relation
\Tr_{H^{*}}(\chi_{\pi}\chi_{\pi^{\prime}})=\delta_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}\dim H
for normalised characters
\chi_{\pi}=\Tr_{\pi}/\dim V_{\pi}
. Moreover, in this case of
H
semisimple,
D(H)
is also, with integrals
\Lambda_{D}=\int\mathop{{\otimes}}\Lambda
and
\int_{D}=\Lambda\mathop{{\otimes}}\int
. Hence the same result applied to
D(H)
tells us that
D(H){\cong}\oplus_{\tilde{\pi}}\mathrm{End}(V_{\tilde{\pi}})
now for irreps
(\tilde{\pi},V_{\tilde{\pi}})
of
D(H)
. Hence our ideas about Bell states and ribbon teleportation still apply in this case, with quasiparticles detected by projectors
P_{\tilde{\pi}}
.

Also note that a representation of
D(H)
can also be described as a
H
-crossed
[
Maj95
,
Maj02
]
or ‘Drinfeld-Yetter’ module consisting of a left action or representation
\pi
of
H
and a compatible
left coaction
of
H

\Delta_{L}
(this is equivalent to a compatible right action of
H^{*}
or left action of
H^{*op}
on the same vector space, these being two subalgebras from which
D(H)
is built). If
V_{\tilde{\pi}}
has basis
\{e_{i}\}
then the structures for a crossed module are
\Delta_{L}e_{i}=\rho_{ij}\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{j}
where
a{\triangleright}e_{i}=\langle a,\rho_{ij}\rangle e_{j}
is the corresponding action, and
h{\triangleright}e_{i}=\pi(h)_{ki}e_{k}
as usual. We sum over the repeated
k
and
\rho_{ij}\in H
is required to obey

\Delta\rho_{ij}=\rho_{ik}\mathop{{\otimes}}\rho_{kj},\quad{\epsilon}\rho_{ij}=\delta_{ij},\quad h_{1}\rho_{ik}\pi(h_{2})_{jk}=\pi(h_{1})_{ki}\rho_{kj}h_{2}

for all
h\in H
, again summing over
k
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
S^{2}=\text{id}
and
\tilde{\pi}
an irrep of
D(H)
with
\pi,\rho
its associated crossed module data with respect to a basis
\{e_{i}\}
as above. Then

P_{\tilde{\pi}}=\dim(V_{\tilde{\pi}})\sum_{a,i,j}f^{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}\Lambda_{1}\pi(S\Lambda_{2})_{ij}(\int e_{a}S\rho_{ij}).

Moreover, when specialised to
D(G)
, we recover the projectors
P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}
in (
9
).

Proof.

The general formula for the tensor product integral becomes

\displaystyle P_{\tilde{\pi}}

\displaystyle=\dim(V_{\tilde{\pi}})\int_{1}\mathop{{\otimes}}\Lambda_{1}\Tr_{\tilde{\pi}}((S\int_{2})S\Lambda_{2})=\dim(V_{\tilde{\pi}})f^{a}\int_{1}e_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}\Lambda_{1}\langle S\int_{2},\rho_{kj}\rangle\langle f^{i},e_{j}\rangle\pi(S\Lambda_{2})_{ki}

which becomes as stated using Hopf algebra duality. In the
D(G)
case, we let
\tilde{\pi}=(\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi)
as before and go back to (
23
). Then

\displaystyle P_{\tilde{\pi}}

\displaystyle={\dim(V_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi})\over|G|}\sum_{h,g\in G}(\delta_{h}\mathop{{\otimes}}g)\Tr_{\tilde{\pi}}(S(\delta_{h^{-1}}\mathop{{\otimes}}g))

\displaystyle={\dim V_{\pi}\over|C_{G}|}\sum_{h,g\in G}\sum_{c\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},i}(\delta_{h}\mathop{{\otimes}}g)\langle\delta_{c}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{i},(\delta_{g^{-1}hg}\mathop{{\otimes}}g^{-1}){\triangleright}(c\mathop{{\otimes}}e_{i})\rangle

\displaystyle={\dim V_{\pi}\over|C_{G}|}\sum_{h,g\in G}\sum_{c\in\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}}(\delta_{h}\mathop{{\otimes}}g)\delta_{g^{-1}hg,g^{-1}cg}\delta_{c,g^{-1}cg}\Tr_{\pi}(q^{-1}_{g^{-1}cg}g^{-1}q_{c})=P_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}},\pi}

where
{\triangleright}
is the action (
13
). We view the restrictions setting
h=c
and
g\in C_{G}(c)
. Changing variables to
n=q_{c}gq_{c}^{-1}
, this is equivalent to a sum over
n\in C_{G}(r_{\hbox{{$\mathcal{C}$}}})
as usual and
n^{-1}
in the trace. ∎

How exactly to construct and classify irreps
\tilde{\pi}
, however, depends on the structure of
D(H)
, which is no longer generally a semidirect product. This therefore has to be handled on a case by case basis before one can do practical quantum computations.

Example 3.2
.

Let
G=G_{+}.G_{-}
be a finite group that factorises into two subgroups
G_{\pm}
, neither of which need be normal and
H=\mathbb{C}(G_{-}){\blacktriangleright\!\!\triangleleft}\mathbb{C}G_{+}
the associated bicrossproduct (or ‘bismash product’) quantum group
[
Tak81
,
Maj90
,
Maj95
]
, which is semisimple. It is shown in
[
BGM96
]
that
D(H){\cong}D(G)_{F}
, where the latter is a Drinfeld twist of
D(G)
by a 2-cocycle

F=\sum_{g\in G}1\mathop{{\otimes}}g_{-}\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g^{-1}}\mathop{{\otimes}}1\in D(G)\mathop{{\otimes}}D(G)

in the sense of
[
Maj95
]
. We write
g=g_{+}g_{-}
for the unique factorisation of any element of
G
. Explicitly,
D(G)_{F}
has the same algebra as
D(G)
but a conjugated coproduct

\Delta(\delta_{g}\mathop{{\otimes}}h)=F(\Delta_{D(G)}(\delta_{g}\mathop{{\otimes}}h))F^{-1}=\sum_{f\in G}\delta_{f_{-}gff_{-}^{-1}}\mathop{{\otimes}}f_{-}h(h^{-1}fh)_{-}^{-1}\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f^{-1}}\mathop{{\otimes}}h

after a short computation. The nontrivial isomorphism with
D(H)
in
[
BGM96
]
is needed to identify the
H
and
H^{*op}
subalgebras but where this is not required, we can work directly with this twisted description. In particular, irreps of
D(G)_{F}
are the same as those of
D(G)
(since the algebra is not changed) and can be identified with irreps of
D(H)
by the isomorphism. The braided tensor category is different from but monoidally equivalent to that of
D(G)
.

We will be concerned more with the formalism with explicit models, such as based on this construction, deferred to a sequel. We see, however, that there are plenty of examples. Note that
G{\triangleright\!\!\!<}G
by Ad is an example with one subgroup normal, so
H=D(G)
is covered by this analysis and
D(D(G)){\cong}D(G{\triangleright\!\!\!<}G)_{F}
.

1
D(H)
site operators.

By working with the above cleaner form of the Drinfeld double, our modest new observation in this section is that the same format for the Kitaev model works in the general case without assuming
S^{2}=\text{id}
provided we use additional information from the lattice geometry to distinguish the four cases (a)-(d) in Figure
3
which follow the same rules as above but sometimes specify to use
S^{-1}
. We focus on the case of a square lattice with its standard orientation as this is most relevant to computer science, rather than on a general ciliated ribbon graph.

Figure 3
:
Kitaev model representation of
D(H)
at a site
(v,p)
for general not necessarily semisimple Hopf algebras. Most instances of the antipode
S
can be equally
S^{-1}
but if we use
S
in all the
a{\triangleright}
then we have to use
S^{-1}
in
h{\triangleright}
if this occurs in the first arrow encountered in going around the vertex.

Theorem 3.3
.

If
(v,p)
is a site in the lattice then the actions for the form in Figure
3
, where we act as shown and by the identity on other arrows, is a representation of
D(H)
provided we use (as shown)
S^{-1}
if the first arrow going around the vertex is inward and
S
if the last arrow is inward. We can freely choose
S
or
S^{-1}
if the inward arrow is in one of the intermediate places.

Proof.

We have to check the relations
a_{2}h_{2}\langle Sh_{1},a_{1}\rangle\langle h_{3},a_{3}\rangle=ha
for all
h\in H
and
a\in H^{*}
. The proof of the hardest case (c) is in Figure
4
and for the cancellation for the 3rd equality, we see that we need
S^{-1}
when the first vertex going around is inward and
S
when the last vertex is, as is the case here. The other cases are similar but slightly easier as unconstrained on the choice of
S
where there is no inward arrow in one or both of these positions. ∎

Figure 4
:
Proof that case (c) of Figure
3
works in Theorem
3.3
.

We could equally well decide to always use
S
for
h{\triangleright}
and use
S^{-1}
in
a{\triangleright}
if the contraflowing is in first position going around the face and
S
if it is in last position. (This is the same as above in the dual lattice and faces and arrows interchanged and with the roles of
H,H^{*}
interchanged.)
We see that in the non-involutive
S
case there is still some freedom in the choice of
S
or
S^{-1}
, which we need to fix by what we want to do with these
D(H)
-representations.

We also know that our finite dimensional Hopf algebra has up to scale a unique right integral
\Lambda\in H
and a unique right-invariant integral
\int:H\to k
so we can proceed to define operators

A(v,p)=\Lambda{\triangleright},\quad B(v,p)=\int{\triangleright}

on
\mathcal{H}
. It is striking that exactly this integral data is also key to a Frobenius Hopf algebra interacting pair for ZX calculus based on
H
, see
[
CD19
,
Maj21
]
at this level of generality. Clearly

A(v,p)^{2}={\epsilon}(\Lambda)A(v,p),\quad B(v,p)^{2}=(\int 1)B(v,p)

but without further assumptions, both operators depend on both parts of the site. One can also check that

[A(v,p),A(v^{\prime},p^{\prime})]=0,\quad[B(v,p),B(v^{\prime},p^{\prime})]=0

for all
v,v^{\prime},p,p^{\prime}
with in the first case
v\neq v^{\prime}
and in the second case
p\neq p^{\prime}
. The first is because if, in the worst case, the vertices are adjacent then the common arrow is pointing in for one vertex and out for the other, hence the element
g
in the middle gets multiplied by something on the left and something on the right, which does not depend on the order by associativity. Similarly for two faces with an arrow in common. We do
not
in general have that
[A(v,p),B(v,p)]=0
.

For the Hamiltonian, there are two possible approaches. (i) we could we fix the vertex of all site to be at the bottom left of the face (Case (a) in Figure
3
). Thus if
v
is a vertex, we define
p_{v}
as the face to its upper right. Then

H=\sum_{v}(1-A(v,p_{v})+1-B(v,p_{v}))

makes sense. (ii) Alternatively, motivated by
[
Meu17
]
we can define

H_{K}=\prod_{(v,p)}A(v,p)B(v,p)

The
D(G)
model admits a Hamiltonian which is necessarily frustration-free, meaning that any vacuum state is also the lowest energy state of any given local term. This condition is broken by general
D(H)
models. Let
A(v_{1},p_{1})
be a local term. First, consider the Hamiltonian from (i), ignoring the additive constant:

\displaystyle A(v_{1},

\displaystyle p_{v_{1}})|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle=-A(v_{1},p_{v_{1}})\sum_{v}(A(v,p_{v})+B(v,p_{v}))|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-({\epsilon}(\Lambda)A(v_{1},p_{v_{1}})+A(v_{1},p_{v_{1}})B(v_{1},p_{v_{1}})+\sum_{v\neq v_{1}}(A(v,p_{v})+B(v,p_{v})))|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

So in general,
A(v_{1},p_{v_{1}})|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle\neq|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
. Next, consider the Hamiltonian from (ii):

\displaystyle A(v_{1},p_{v_{1}})|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

\displaystyle=A(v_{1},p_{v_{1}})\prod_{(v,p)}A(v,p)B(v,p)|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle={\epsilon}(\Lambda)|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle

The fact that the integral actions are no longer idempotent also breaks the interpretation of these actions as ‘check operators’ to be measured and detect unwanted excitations. In these more general models, it is unclear what error-correcting capabilities still exist on the lattice, or whether there are alternative methods of preserving fault-tolerance. They don’t appear to fit under the umbrella of ‘surface codes’ in the usual sense.
We still preserve some locality as a feature of the model, in the sense that a locally vacuum state can be defined for example in case (ii) as being the image of
\prod_{(v\in V_{R},p\in P_{R})}A(v,p)B(v,p)
, where
V_{R}
and
P_{R}
are sets of vertices and faces in some region
R
.

In the semisimple case where
S^{2}=\text{id}
, we have already noted that
\Delta\Lambda=\mathrm{flip}\Delta\Lambda
and that the integrals can be normalised so that
{\epsilon}(\Lambda)=\int 1=1
. This implies that
A(v,p)=A(v)
independently of
p
and
B(v,p)=B(p)
independently of
v
, are projectors and, using the commutation relations of
D(H)
and Theorem
3.3
that
[A(v),B(p)]=0
. Therefore, we recover both the frustration-free property of
H
and the interpretation of the lattice as a fault-tolerant quantum memory. In this case, it is claimed in
[
Meu17
]
that

H_{K}=\prod_{v}A(v)\circ\prod_{p}B(p),\quad\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}_{vac}=\mathrm{image}(H_{K})

results in the latter ‘protected space’ being a topological invariant of the surface obtained by gluing discs on the faces of a ribbon graph. This motivates the definition above. It is also claimed in
[
Meu17
]
that a particle at
(v,p)
corresponds to a defect where we leave out the site
(v,p)
in the product.

While the
D(G)
model on a lattice
\Sigma
allows for the convenient expression in Theorem
2.2
for
\dim(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}_{vac})
in terms of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, the proof of this relies on the invertibility of group elements and the invariance under orientation of
\delta_{e}{\triangleright}
. The topological content for
D(H)
models can similarly be related to holonomy as in
[
Meu17
]
but is more complicated. The topological content in the
D(H)
model in the non-semisimple case is less clear and will be more indirect. For example, reversal of orientation cannot be expressed simply via the antipode as this no longer squares to the identity.

2
D(H)
triangle and ribbon operators

Canonical representations of
D(H)
that we will need are left and right actions of
D(H)
on
H
,
[
Maj95
, Ex. 7.1.8]

h{\triangleright}g=h_{1}gSh_{2},\quad a{\triangleright}g=a(g_{1})g_{2};\quad g{\triangleleft}h=(Sh_{1})gh_{2},\quad g{\triangleleft}a=g_{1}a(g_{2})

(24)

and left and right actions of
D(H)
on
H^{*}
,

h{\triangleright}b=\langle Sh,b_{1}\rangle b_{2},\quad a{\triangleright}b=(S^{-1}a_{2})ba_{1};\quad b{\triangleleft}h=b_{1}\langle Sh,b_{2}\rangle,\quad b{\triangleleft}a=a_{2}bS^{-1}a_{1}

(25)

which is essentially the same construction with the roles of
H,H^{*op}
swapped. Moreover, in the quasitriangular case, there is a braided monoidal functor
{}_{H}\hbox{{$\mathcal{M}$}}\to{}_{D(H)}\hbox{{$\mathcal{M}$}}
, see
[
Maj95
,
Maj02
]
, which can be used to obtain a class of nice representations of
D(H)
from irreps of
H
.

Also note that if
D
is any Hopf algebra, for example
D=D(H)
, it acts on its dual as a module algebra by the left and right coregular representations

d{\triangleright}\phi=\langle Sd,\phi_{1}\rangle\phi_{2},\quad\phi{\triangleleft}d=\phi_{1}\langle Sd,\phi_{2}\rangle

(26)

for all
d\in D
and
\phi\in D^{*}
. These already feature in (
25
) for the action of
H
. Also, if
D
acts from the left (say) on a vector space
\mathcal{H}
by an action
{\triangleright}
then it acts on the linear operators
\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
as a module algebra from both the left and the right
[
Maj95
]
.

(d{\triangleright}L)(\psi)=d_{1}{\triangleright}L(Sd_{2}{\triangleright}\psi),\quad(L{\triangleleft}d)(\psi)=Sd_{1}{\triangleright}L(d_{2}{\triangleright}\psi)

(27)

for all
d
in the Hopf algebra and
L\in\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
. We will use (
27
) with different site actions of
D(H)
in Theorem
3.3
for example
{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}
and
{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}
for the two halves. These commute if
s_{0},s_{1}
are far enough apart. In the case of
D(H)
, its dual is
H\mathop{{\otimes}}H^{*}
as an algebra and has the coproduct

\Delta_{D(H)^{*}}(h\mathop{{\otimes}}a)=\sum_{a,b}h_{2}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{a}a_{1}f^{b}\mathop{{\otimes}}Se_{a}h_{1}e_{b}\mathop{{\otimes}}a_{2}.

(28)

Next, we define Hopf algebra triangle and ribbon operators at least in the case
S^{2}=\text{id}
.
Before attempting this, we need to better understand the
D(G)
case, both ribbon covariance properties and the construction of a ribbon a sequence of triangle and dual triangle ops as defined in Definition
2.9
.

Lemma 3.4
.

For
D(G)
and with left and right actions on
\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
induced as in (
27
) by the initial and final site actions:

1.

If
\tau^{*}
is a dual triangle, the triangle operator
L_{\tau*}^{h}=\sum_{g}F_{\tau^{*}}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}
is a left and right module map
L_{\tau^{*}}:\mathbb{C}G\to\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
, where
D(G)
acts as in (
24
) by

(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}h=\delta_{a,bhb^{-1}}bhb^{-1},\quad h{\triangleleft}(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b)=b^{-1}hb\,\delta_{a,h}.

2.

If
\tau
is a direct triangle, the triangle operator
T_{\tau}^{\delta_{g}}=F_{\tau}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}
for any
h
is a left and right module map
T_{\tau}:\mathbb{C}(G)\to\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
, where
D(G)
acts as in (
25
) by

(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}\delta_{g}=\delta_{a,e}\delta_{bg},\quad\delta_{g}{\triangleleft}(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b)=\delta_{a,e}\delta_{gb}.

3.

If
\xi
is an open ribbon then
\tilde{F}_{\xi}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}:=F_{\xi}^{h^{-1},g}
is a left and right module map
\tilde{F}:D(G)^{*}\to\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
, where
D(G)
acts by (
26
). Moreover, if
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
are composeable ribbons then

\tilde{F}_{\xi^{\prime}\circ\xi}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}=\tilde{F}_{\xi^{\prime}}^{(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g})_{2}}\circ\tilde{F}_{\xi}^{(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g})_{1}}

using the coproduct (
28
) of
D(G)^{*}
. This also applies to
F_{\xi}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}=F_{\xi}^{h,g}
.

Proof.

(1) The relations we find for dual triangles are

(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{0}}\circ\tilde{F}^{b^{-1}hb\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}=\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}\circ(\delta_{ha}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{0}},\quad(\delta_{ah}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{1}}\circ\tilde{F}^{b^{-1}hb\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}=\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}\circ(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{1}}

which we interpret as stated.

(2) The relations we find for direct triangles are

(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{0}}\circ\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}=\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{bg}}\circ(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{0}},\quad\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}\circ(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{1}}=(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{1}}\circ\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{gb}}

which we interpret as stated. These same commutation rules hold for the action
{\triangleright}_{t}
at any site
t
that has the same vertex as
s_{0},s_{1}
respectively, while the action at other sites commutes with the triangle operator.

(3) Here
D(G)^{*}=\mathbb{C}G\mathop{{\otimes}}\mathbb{C}(G)
as an algebra while its coproduct dual to the product of
D(G)
is

\Delta_{D(G)^{*}}(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g})=\sum_{f\in G}h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f}\mathop{{\otimes}}f^{-1}hf\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f^{-1}g}

Then the composition rule in equation (
15
) for
F_{\xi}
is already in the form stated. The same then applies
\tilde{F}_{\xi}
as
S\mathop{{\otimes}}\text{id}
is clearly a coalgebra map. For equivariance, we have from Lemma
2.8
,

\displaystyle\langle S(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b)_{1},(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g})_{1}\rangle\tilde{F}_{\xi}^{(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}){}_{(2)}}\circ(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b)_{1}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,f}\langle S(\delta_{x^{-1}}\mathop{{\otimes}}b),h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f}\rangle\tilde{F}_{\xi}^{f^{-1}hf\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f^{-1}g}}\circ(\delta_{xa}\mathop{{\otimes}}b)_{1}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,f}\langle\delta_{b^{-1}xb}\mathop{{\otimes}}b^{-1},h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f}\rangle\tilde{F}_{\xi}^{f^{-1}hf\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f^{-1}g}}\circ(\delta_{xa}\mathop{{\otimes}}b)_{1}{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{F}_{\xi}^{bhb^{-1}\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{fg}}\circ(\delta_{bhb^{-1}a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{0}}=\delta_{a}{\triangleright}\tilde{F}_{\xi}^{bhb^{-1}\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{bg}}\circ b{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle=(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{0}}\tilde{F}_{\xi}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}

where
f=b^{-1}
and
x=bhb^{-1}
. We used the commutation relations from Lemma
2.8
. Similarly for the other side,

\displaystyle(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b)

{}_{1}{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}\circ\tilde{F}^{(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g})_{1}}_{\xi}\langle S(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){}_{(2)},(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}){}_{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,f}(\delta_{ax}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{1}}\circ\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f}}_{\xi}\langle S(\delta_{x^{-1}}\mathop{{\otimes}}b),f^{-1}hf\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f^{-1}g}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,f}(\delta_{ax}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{1}}\circ\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f}}_{\xi}\langle\delta_{b^{-1}xb}\mathop{{\otimes}}b^{-1},f^{-1}hf\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f^{-1}g}\rangle

\displaystyle=(\delta_{ag^{-1}hg}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{1}}\circ\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{gb}}_{\xi}=\delta_{ag^{-1}hg}{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}\circ\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}_{\xi}\circ b{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}=\tilde{F}_{\xi}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}\circ(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{1}}

where
gb=f
and
x=bf^{-1}hfb^{-1}=g^{-1}hg
.
∎

The additional commutation relations for
T_{\tau}
mentioned in the proof can best be said as the action on it as an operator in
\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
,

(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{t}(T^{\delta_{g}})=T^{(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{t}\delta_{g}};\quad(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{t}\delta_{g}=\delta_{a,e}\begin{cases}\delta_{bg}\\
\delta_{gb^{-1}}\\
\delta_{g}\end{cases}

where we act as per
L^{b}
at vertex
t
on
g
regarded effectively as living on the arrow of the direct triangle, i.e.
bg
if the arrow in relation to the vertex of
t
is outgoing,
gb^{-1}
if incoming and
g
otherwise. We can then derive the left and right module properties for ribbon operators by iterating those for triangle operators. To illustrate this, consider

\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}_{\tau_{2}\circ\tau_{1}^{*}}=T_{\tau_{2}}^{\delta_{g}}\circ L_{\tau_{1}^{*}}^{h^{-1}}=\sum_{f}\tilde{F}_{\tau_{2}}^{f^{-1}hf\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f^{-1}g}}\circ\tilde{F}_{\tau_{1}^{*}}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f}}=(\tilde{F}_{\tau_{2}}\circ\tilde{F}_{\tau_{1}^{*}})(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g})

where
\tau_{1}^{*}:s_{0}\to s_{1}
and
\tau_{2}:s_{1}\to s_{2}
and
\tilde{F}_{\tau}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}=T_{\tau}^{\delta_{g}}
and
\tilde{F}_{\tau^{*}}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}=\delta_{g,e}L_{\tau^{*}}^{h^{-1}}
are the associated ribbon operators which we convolve as in part (3) of the lemma. Using the first expression and the triangle operator left module properties

\displaystyle(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{0}}(\tilde{F}_{\tau_{2}\circ\tau_{1}^{*}}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}})

\displaystyle=(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b)_{1}{\triangleright}(T_{\tau_{2}}^{\delta_{g}})\circ(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b)_{2}{\triangleright}(L_{\tau_{1}^{*}}^{h^{-1}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}T_{\tau_{2}}^{(\delta_{ax^{-1}}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}_{s_{0}}\delta_{g}}\circ L_{\tau_{1}^{*}}^{(\delta_{x}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}h^{-1}}

\displaystyle=T_{\tau_{2}}^{\delta_{bg}}\circ L_{\tau_{1}^{*}}^{\delta_{a,bh^{-1}b^{-1}}bh^{-1}b^{-1}}=\delta_{a^{-1},bhb^{-1}}\tilde{F}_{\tau_{2}\circ\tau_{1}^{*}}^{bhb^{-1}\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{bg}}=\tilde{F}_{\tau_{2}\circ\tau_{1}^{*}}^{(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g})}

where from (
26
),

(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b){\triangleright}(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g})=\sum_{f}\langle\delta_{b^{-1}a^{-1}b}\mathop{{\otimes}}b^{-1},h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f}\rangle f^{-1}hf\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f^{-1}g}=\delta_{h,b^{-1}a^{-1}b}bhb^{-1}\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{bg}

The similar calculation for
(\tilde{F}_{\tau_{2}\circ\tau_{1}^{*}}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}){\triangleleft}_{s_{2}}(\delta_{a}\mathop{{\otimes}}b)
is not so easy as
{\triangleleft}_{s_{2}}
does not enjoy simple commutation relations with
L^{h^{-1}}
. There is a similar story for

\tilde{F}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g}}_{\tau_{2}^{*}\circ\tau_{1}}=L_{\tau_{2}^{*}}^{g^{-1}h^{-1}g}\circ T_{\tau_{1}}^{\delta_{g}}=\sum_{f}\tilde{F}_{\tau_{2}^{*}}^{f^{-1}hf\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f^{-1}g}}\circ\tilde{F}_{\tau_{1}}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{f}}=(\tilde{F}_{\tau_{2}^{*}}\circ\tilde{F}_{\tau_{1}})(h\mathop{{\otimes}}\delta_{g})

as the other smallest open ribbon.

Now proceeding to the Hopf algebra case, we define triangle operations in the obvious manner as partial vertex and face operators, with left multiplication by
h
or right multiplication by
S^{\pm 1}h
for dual triangles, depending on orientation,

Recall that we have chosen to use
S
throughout the face operations but
S^{-1}
if the first arrow was inward in a vertex operation. As a result, we need both versions
{}^{(\pm)}L^{h}_{\tau^{*}}
to express both left and right covariance. We could equally well have put this complication on the
T^{a}_{\tau}
side.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
H
be a finite-dimensional Hopf algebra,
D(H)
act on
H
,
H^{*}
as in (
24
) and (
25
) and act on
\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
as in (
27
) from the left induced by
{\triangleright}_{s_{0}}
and from the right induced by
{\triangleright}_{s_{1}}
.

1.

For dual triangles,
{}^{(-)}L_{\tau^{*}}:H\to\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
is a left module map and
{}^{(+)}L_{\tau^{*}}:H\to\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
is right module map.

2.

The direct triangle operator
T_{\tau}:H^{*}\to\mathrm{End}(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}})
is a left and right module map.

Proof.

This is shown in Figure
5
and Figure
6
for sample orientations where
S^{\pm}
appears in the dual triangle operation. The other orientations are similar with less work.
We use the definitions and the actions of
D(H)
on
H
and
H^{*}
.
∎

:
(a) Proof of left covariance of dual triangle operator needing the
(-)
version for the 3rd equality. (b) Proof of right covariance needing the
(+)
version. They coincide when
S^{2}=\text{id}
.

:
Proof of (a) left covariance and (b) right covariance of direct triangle operator.

Next, we define ribbon operators
F^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}a}_{\xi}
associated to a ribbon
\xi
by convolution-composition of triangle operations, where
h\in H
and
a\in H^{*}
. They are a special case of the ‘holonomy’ maps defined in
[
Meu17
]
but even so, it is nontrivial to write them out explicitly in our case and in our notations. The first step it to view triangle operators as ribbon operators by

\tilde{F}_{\tau}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}a}={\epsilon}(h)T_{\tau}^{a},\quad{}^{(\pm)}\tilde{F}_{\tau^{*}}^{h\mathop{{\otimes}}a}={\epsilon}(a){}^{(\pm)}L_{\tau^{*}}^{S^{-1}h}.

(29)

Next, the ribbon operators for two composeable ribbons can be convolution-composed by

\tilde{F}_{\xi^{\prime}\circ\xi}^{\phi}=\tilde{F}_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\phi_{2}}\circ\tilde{F}_{\xi}^{\phi_{1}}

(30)

where now we use the coproduct (
28
) on
\phi\in D(H)^{*}
. This is an associative operation, so starting with a triangle operation viewed as a ribbon operator and extending to a ribbon by composing a series of these, we correspondingly define the associated ribbon operator by iterating this formula. Because
{\epsilon}\mathop{{\otimes}}\text{id}
and
\text{id}\mathop{{\otimes}}{\epsilon}
are coalgebra maps from
D(H)^{*}
to
H^{*},H^{cop}
respectively, the convolution of direct triangle operators viewed as ribbons is the same as convolution of
T
s via the coproduct of
H^{*}
and (due to the
S^{-1}
) the convolution of dual triangle operators as ribbons is the same as convolution of
L
s via the coproduct of
H
. It follows that the
D(H)
site actions in Theorem
3.3
can be viewed as ribbon operators, where for our default conventions
a{\triangleright}=T^{a_{4}}\circ\cdots\circ T^{a_{1}}
going clockwise around a face and
h{\triangleright}={}^{(+)}L^{h_{4}}\circ\cdots{}^{(+)}L^{h_{2}}\circ{}^{(-)}L^{h_{1}}
going anticlockwise around a vertex. The sign refers to the use of
S^{\pm 1}
if applicable and as noted, we can also have different patterns of signs, including in the
T
’s, and still get an action of
D(H)
.

In particular, this means that we no longer have a clear route to topological invariance as we can construct a contractible, closed ribbon equal to
A(v,p)
(or
B(v,p)
).
A(v,p)|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle\neq|{\mathrm{vac}}\rangle
in general, so ribbon operators are no longer invariant up to isotopy. As a consequence, it is not clear that
\dim(\hbox{{$\mathcal{H}$}}_{vac})
is a topological invariant in general, albeit it is known to be one in the semisimple case.

Next, we wish to prove a generalisation of Lemma
2.8
from Section
2
. However, we could previously rely on topological invariance to justify claims in our proofs by bending ribbons into a convenient shape and eliminating contractible loops.
In the non-semisimple case, we need to specify a new class of ribbon for which our generalisation applies and where these steps are not needed.

Definition 3.6
.

Recall that a ribbon is a sequence of triangles between sites. Let us represent it as a list of sites in order
[s_{0},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}]:=[(v_{0},p_{0}),(v_{1},p_{1}),\cdots,(v_{n},p_{n})]
, which must change either the vertex or face between each site.
A
strongly open
ribbon
\xi
is an open ribbon which satisfies the following condition: any two sites
s_{i}:=(v_{i},p_{i})
,
s_{j}:=(v_{j},p_{j})
inside
\xi
may have
v_{i}=v_{j}
only if every site in the sequence of sites
[s_{i+1},\cdots,s_{j-1}]
between
s_{i},s_{j}
also has

v_{i+1}=\cdots=v_{j-1}
. Similarly,
p_{i}=p_{j}
is only allowed if
p_{i+1}=\cdots=p_{j-1}
.

The intuition behind this is that the ribbon
\xi
does not bend ‘too quickly’ or get ‘too close’ to itself; equivalently, it is saying that all subribbons of
\xi
are either on a single vertex/face or are themselves open.

s_{n}

(a)

s_{0}
s_{n}
s_{2}
s_{3}

(b)

s_{0}
s_{2}
s_{3}
s_{n}

(c)

Figure 7
:
(A) is a strongly open ribbon. (B) and (C) are open, but not strongly open.

.

Figure
7(a)
shows a strongly open ribbon. While it has rotations at a vertex and a face, it never returns to previously seen vertices or faces. However, Figure
7(b)
is an open ribbon which is not strongly open: at the self-crossing of the ribbon, there are sites
s_{2}
,
s_{3}
such that
s_{2}=s_{3}
but they are not sequentially adjacent in the ribbon.
Similarly, Figure
7(c)
is an open ribbon which does not cross itself but gets ‘too close’ – sites
s_{4}
,
s_{5}
intersect at
p_{4}
.

.
